# The funding band

The programme map splits the 135 nodes closest to the alleged programme into seven bands, and this file is the funding band: 22 nodes, taken era by era from the earliest to the most recent. Each entry gives what the node is, why the board places it here, why it holds the tier it holds, what it has denied, its documented and testimonial claims with their sources, and the links that tie it to the rest of the board.

Names in full, for this file:

- **INSCOM**: US Army Intelligence and Security Command
- **NIDS**: National Institute for Discovery Science

# THROUGH 1969: Estimate through Blue Book

## National Reconnaissance Office

National Reconnaissance Office is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1961; axes C2 A3 D2; 13 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: Overhead reconnaissance agency whose existence was itself classified for 31 years.

### What it is

The National Reconnaissance Office is the joint Department of Defense / Intelligence Community agency that designs, builds, launches and operates United States reconnaissance satellites. Management arrangements creating it and the National Reconnaissance Program were signed on 6 September 1961 by Acting DCI General Charles P. Cabell and Deputy Secretary of Defense Roswell L. Gilpatric. Its very existence was classified for thirty-one years and was declassified only on 18 September 1992.

### Why it is implicated

The NRO is the existence proof for the central premise of this board: that the United States can operate a multi-billion-dollar technical enterprise for three decades with no public existence, no line item Congress could disaggregate, and no acknowledged budget. When it was finally pried open in August 1994 it emerged that the Senate Select Committee on Intelligence did not know the NRO had built a one-million-square-foot, $310 million headquarters in Chantilly, Virginia, because the money sat in the "base" portion of the budget rather than as a new initiative. By 1996 the agency was found to be sitting on billions of dollars in forward funding that had not been reported to CIA, the Pentagon, or Congress. It is also the agency that produced David Grusch, who served as the NRO's representative to the Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force from 2019 to 2021 and whose subsequent complaint alleged a concealed multi-decade recovery and reverse-engineering effort. Finally, the NRO's own 1992 declassification memorandum is a template artifact of the concealment posture: it declassified the fact of the agency and then closed with a single sentence refusing everything else.

### The longer account

Everything about the NRO is instructive for a board that asks not whether but how. Start with the birth certificate: not a statute, not a public charter, but management arrangements agreed on 6 September 1961 between the Acting Director of Central Intelligence and the Deputy Secretary of Defense, consolidating Air Force, CIA and Navy overhead reconnaissance programs into a single compartmented office. For thirty-one years the fact of the office was itself classified. It hired, contracted, launched, and spent, and the American public and most of the Congress did not know the name.

The 18 September 1992 declassification is worth reading closely because it is a study in minimum viable disclosure. Deputy Secretary of Defense Donald Atwood announced, in Memorandum for Correspondents No. 264-M, that there is an NRO, that it is an agency of the Department of Defense, that it is funded through the National Reconnaissance Program, that its mission is spaceborne and airborne collection, and that the Director reports directly to the Secretary of Defense while the Director of Central Intelligence sets collection priorities and jointly approves the budget. It named three officials, Faga, Hill, March, and gave a Pentagon mailing address and a phone number. Then it ended: "No other information relating to the National Reconnaissance Office was declassified." That is the entire architecture of managed disclosure in one sentence. Acknowledge the container; classify the contents.

The 1994 headquarters affair is the second lesson. On 8 August 1994 members of the Senate Select Committee on Intelligence held a press conference to disclose the existence of a secret $310 million headquarters complex under construction in Chantilly, Virginia, and to attack the intelligence community for not properly informing them of its size and cost. Two days later DCI James Woolsey produced a paper trail sufficient to argue there had been no deliberate concealment, but the paper trail was not the point. The point, as the Federation of American Scientists documented at the time, was that the project had been buried in the "base" part of the budget rather than broken out as a new initiative, and that base programs go essentially unscrutinized. The NRO's own headquarters project director, Roger Marsh, conceded in the agency's first open hearing: "We have been negligent, clearly negligent, for not showing the budget breakout for this project." Senator Daniel Patrick Moynihan was blunter: "This is an agency which has lied to Congress before. Egregiously." Senator John Warner put the resource asymmetry on the record, the NRO had twenty-five people working budget matters; the Senate committee had one. The NRO's own share of the intelligence budget at the time was roughly $6.5 billion.

The third lesson is the money itself. Reporting in 1995 and 1996 established that the NRO had accumulated a very large pool of unspent forward funding, figures reported in the range of $1 billion to $1.7 billion initially, and ultimately around $3.8 billion, without informing CIA, the Pentagon or Congress. NRO Director Jeffrey K. Harris departed in February 1996. The relevance to a legacy recovery program is direct and mechanical: an agency inside the classified budget can accumulate carryover at a scale that would fund an entire concealed enterprise, and the discovery of that carryover required a scandal, not oversight.

The fourth lesson is personnel. David Grusch served as the NRO's representative to the Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force from 2019 to 2021, and afterward as the National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency's co-lead for UAP analysis and its task-force representative into July 2022. His allegations, published by Leslie Kean and Ralph Blumenthal in The Debrief on 5 June 2023 and repeated under oath before the House Oversight Committee on 26 July 2023, did not originate with a UFO enthusiast off the street. They originated with the officer the NRO sent to the interagency table. Whatever one concludes about the substance, the provenance runs through this agency.

What the NRO has not done is speak to UAP at all. It has issued no denial, no confirmation, no historical review. It is not named in AARO's public historical reporting as a subject of interest. It has never been asked in open session what it holds. Under sections 1841-1843 of the FY2024 NDAA the NRO is a "Government office" like any other and was obliged to review, identify and organize its UAP records for transmission to the National Archives by 20 October 2024; there is no public accounting of what it transmitted or withheld, and under section 1843 the postponement decision rests with its own original classification authority.

For an analyst, the NRO is best understood not as a suspect but as a calibration instrument. It tells you the maximum duration of full concealment achievable in the modern American system (thirty-one years), the mechanism (base-budget aggregation inside a compartmented program), the failure mode (a construction project too physically large to hide), the discovery mechanism (press and scandal rather than committee), and the recovery posture (declassify the container, refuse the contents). Any hypothesis about a legacy recovery program that requires less institutional capability than the NRO demonstrably possessed is, on the face of it, feasible.

### What it has denied

The NRO has issued no public statement on UAP, no denial and no confirmation, and has never been examined on the subject in open session. Its institutional posture is the one modeled by its own 1992 declassification memorandum: acknowledge the container and refuse everything inside it.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, funding, oversight, research.

### Open questions on this node

- What, if anything, the NRO transmitted to the National Archives under sections 1841-1843, and how many of its records it postponed under section 1843.
- Whether the NRO holds or has held any special access program with UAP equities, and whether any such program is waived under 10 U.S.C. 119(e).
- What Grusch saw or was told in his capacity as NRO representative to the UAP Task Force, as distinct from what he was later told by others.
- Whether the forward-funding practice uncovered in 1995-96 was unique to the NRO or characteristic of compartmented programs generally, and whether any comparable audit has been performed since.
- The identity and disposition of NRO programs historically misidentified in the UAP record, AARO has asserted misattribution of classified U.S. programs generally but has not published an NRO-specific accounting.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 109 of 1159, score 60). 13 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 2 official denials recorded against it. Axes C2/A3/D2.

## United States House of Representatives

United States House of Representatives is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1789-03-04; axes C0 A2 D3; 12 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: Lower chamber; venue of every public UAP hearing since 2023.

### What it is

The United States House of Representatives, and specifically the three bodies within it that have repeatedly touched the legacy program question: the Committee on Armed Services, which held the first open congressional UFO hearing in 1966; the Committee on Oversight and Government Reform, which has run the modern public hearings since 2023; and that committee's Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets, chaired by Rep. Anna Paulina Luna. The House is also the chamber whose FY2024 NDAA conference product created the statutory UAP Records Collection at the National Archives.

### Why it is implicated

The House is not a bystander in this record; it is the institution that has been lied to, and periodically the institution that catches the lie. Its 1966 Armed Services hearing extracted the Air Force's official position on the record from a sitting Secretary and simultaneously put J. Allen Hynek's dissent into the Congressional record. Its Oversight Committee in 2023 put a former Navy pilot and other witnesses in front of cameras under oath. Its members have since gone after the contractor layer directly, issuing legislative interrogatories to The MITRE Corporation and MIT Lincoln Laboratory rather than to executive agencies, which is a deliberate move around the FOIA exemptions and classification authorities that block agency requests. And the House is where the funding lever sits: FY2024 NDAA section 1687 makes UAP-related independent research and development costs unallowable as indirect expenses unless the underlying material is made available to the defense and intelligence committees and to congressional leadership.

### The longer account

The House's involvement runs on a sixty-year cycle of the same three moves: hearing, denial, statute.

Move one, 1966. On April 5, 1966 the Committee on Armed Services of the House of Representatives, 89th Congress, second session, held a hearing on Unidentified Flying Objects, convened after pressure from House Minority Leader Gerald Ford. The Air Force sent Secretary Harold Brown, and Project Blue Book sent Major Hector Quintanilla, its serving chief, and Dr. J. Allen Hynek, its scientific consultant. Brown put the Air Force position on the record: no evidence that UFOs threatened national security or were extraterrestrial. Hynek used the same hearing to place on the record that the Air Force's practice of assigning conventional explanations to every report was a poverty of hypotheses, and to call for an independent civilian scientific panel. The Air Force's response was to fund the Condon study at the University of Colorado, which is the template: a congressional hearing produces not disclosure but an outsourced study whose public summary can be written to a conclusion. That is exactly what had already been done with Battelle and Special Report No. 14 eleven years earlier.

Move two, 2023 to 2026. On July 26, 2023 the Oversight Committee's Subcommittee on National Security, the Border, and Foreign Affairs, chaired by Rep. Glenn Grothman with Rep. Robert Garcia as ranking member, and driven by Reps. Tim Burchett and Anna Paulina Luna, held the first public hearing to take testimony from military pilots and whistleblowers. Ryan Graves's written statement placed three institutional claims into the record: that UAP in U.S. airspace are grossly underreported; that stigma is itself a national security problem; and that excessive classification is hiding what the government knows, with all UAP video classified secret or above since 2021. Graves also entered a specific, dated, attributable account of information suppression: his advisor Rear Admiral Tim Gallaudet received a classified SIPRNET email in 2015 titled 'Urgent safety of flight issue' attaching the GO FAST video, and the following day the email was removed from Gallaudet's system and from his deputy's, with no one ever discussing it again. Gallaudet's own stated presumption is that the removal was connected to a classified special access program. That single paragraph in a House witness statement is the cleanest documented instance of a UAP record being pulled out of an official system.

By 2025 the House had institutionalized the effort. Chairman James Comer created the Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets and named Luna to chair it, with Rep. Jasmine Crockett as ranking member and Republican members Nancy Mace, Tim Burchett, Lauren Boebert, Eric Burlison, Eli Crane and Brandon Gill, and Democratic members Stephen Lynch, Raja Krishnamoorthi and Dave Min. On September 9, 2025 the task force held a hearing on restoring public trust through UAP transparency and whistleblower protection, taking testimony from Air Force veteran Jeffrey Nuccetelli, Chief Alexandro Wiggins, journalist George Knapp and Air Force veteran Dylan Borland.

Move three, statute. The FY2024 National Defense Authorization Act, Public Law 118-31, sections 1841 through 1843, directed NARA to establish the Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Records Collection and required every federal agency to review, identify and organize its UAP records for transmission to the National Archives by October 20, 2024. The statutory definition of what belongs in the collection is the tell: copies of all Government, Government-provided, or Government-funded records relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena, technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence. Congress wrote non-human intelligence into a records statute. NARA's implementing metadata schema then required a mandatory Special Controls field populated with SAP, SCI, RD/FRD and FGI markings, and required the Department of Energy to be entered as a referral agency whenever a file contains Restricted Data or Formerly Restricted Data. The Archives built its intake to receive special-access and nuclear-classified UAP material before anyone had produced any. Separately, section 1687(b) of the same law made UAP-related independent research and development funding unallowable as an indirect expense on covered contracts unless the material is made available to the congressional defense committees, the House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence, the Senate Select Committee on Intelligence, and the four congressional leaders. That provision exists because someone in Congress concluded that contractors were charging the taxpayer for UAP work through overhead while reporting nothing.

By 2026 the House had pivoted to attacking the contractor and FFRDC layer directly. Burlison's May 2026 interrogatories to MITRE, and his earlier letter to MIT Lincoln Laboratory, assert that a nonprofit FFRDC cannot become a private vault for federal records. On August 1, 2026 Burlison released, with administration authorization, preliminary implementation guidance for President Trump's directive on nondisclosure agreements binding current and former government employees and contractors with UAP information, and invited anyone who believes the directive applies to them to contact his office. That is a sitting member of the House offering himself as the intake channel for people whose NDAs may have just been waived. Running alongside it, and not through it, the administration began posting declassified interagency UAP files in May 2026 on war.gov/ufo, an executive release track that answers to no congressional deadline.

### What it has denied

The House does not deny; it is the venue in which denials are entered. The dated, signed executive-branch denials that matter here were delivered to House committees: Secretary of the Air Force Harold Brown's April 5, 1966 statement to Armed Services that no evidence existed of a national security threat or extraterrestrial origin, and the recurring executive position, restated through 2026, that no case has been verified as involving extraterrestrial activity or technology.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure, funding, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- Which agencies actually met the October 20, 2024 UAP Records Collection deadline, and which certified null returns?
- Has any contractor been found in violation of the section 1687(b) IR&D limitation, and has any audit been performed?
- Did any House committee ever receive a waived-SAP notification touching UAP, and would the chair and ranking member be permitted to say so?
- Whether the 1966 Armed Services hearing record contains any internal Air Force acknowledgment of the Special Report No. 14 unknown-rate discrepancy
- What became of the classified briefing Burlison requested from MITRE for Task Force members and cleared committee staff
- How the executive-branch file releases on war.gov/ufo interact with, or bypass, the statutory NARA collection process

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 289 of 1159, score 40). 12 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C0/A2/D3.

- **Tier 1 documented.** Chairwoman Anna Paulina Luna stated on 9 September 2025: 'For a non-committee member to be allowed to view these documents and videos, individual members must be approved by the Chairman and Ranking Member of both HASC and HAC-D.' Source: CHRG-119hhrg61718, 9 September 2025
- **Tier 1 documented.** Chairwoman Luna stated: 'A major barrier to this Committee's inquiry into UAPs has been the lack of cooperation and transparency from the Department of Defense and the intelligence community.' Source: CHRG-119hhrg61718, 9 September 2025
- **Tier 1 documented.** Graves testified that 'Since 2021, all UAP videos are classified as secret or above,' preventing the American people and some Members of Congress from seeing material such as the full GIMBAL video recorded by his squadron. Source: https://oversight.house.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/07/Ryan-HOC-Testimony.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Graves testified that Rear Admiral Tim Gallaudet received a classified SIPRNET email in 2015 titled 'Urgent safety of flight issue' attaching the GO FAST UAP video, that the following day the email was removed from Gallaudet's system and his deputy's, that no one ever discussed it, and that Gallaudet 'presumes the email was removed in connection with a classified special access program.' Source: https://oversight.house.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/07/Ryan-HOC-Testimony.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** House Oversight Chairman James Comer created the Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets and named Rep. Anna Paulina Luna (FL-13) as Chairwoman, with Rep. Jasmine Crockett (TX-30) as Ranking Member; Republican members are Nancy Mace, Tim Burchett, Lauren Boebert, Eric Burlison, Eli Crane and Brandon Gill, and Democratic members are Stephen Lynch, Raja Krishnamoorthi and Dave Min. Source: https://oversight.house.gov/subcommittee/task-force-on-the-declassification-of-federal-secrets/
- **Tier 1 documented.** NARA's metadata requirements for UAP records include a mandatory 'Special Controls' field to be populated with access or handling restrictions such as SAP, SCI, RD/FRD and FGI, and require the Department of Energy to be entered as a referral agency whenever a file contains Restricted Data or Formerly Restricted Data. Source: https://www.archives.gov/records-mgmt/uap-guidance
- **Tier 1 documented.** On April 5, 1966 the Committee on Armed Services of the House of Representatives, 89th Congress second session, held a hearing on Unidentified Flying Objects, with Secretary of the Air Force Harold Brown, Project Blue Book chief Major Hector Quintanilla, and Blue Book scientific consultant Dr. J. Allen Hynek. Source: U.S. House Committee on Armed Services, 'Unidentified Flying Objects,' hearing, 89th Cong., 2d sess., April 5, 1966; https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/CHRG-89hhrg50066O/pdf/CHRG-89hhrg50066O.pdf and https://archive.org/details/ufo_1966_1
- **Tier 1 documented.** On August 1, 2026 Rep. Burlison publicly released, with administration authorization, preliminary implementation guidance for President Trump's directive on nondisclosure agreements covering current and former government employees and contractors with UAP-related information, following his July 21, 2026 statement inviting affected individuals to contact his office. Source: https://burlison.house.gov/media/press-releases/rep-burlison-releases-administration-memorandum-president-trumps-uap-nda (memorandum at https://burlison.house.gov/sites/evo-subsites/burlison.house.gov/files/evo-media-document/memorandum_2026.pdf)
- **Tier 1 documented.** Rep. Eric Burlison, a member of both the Oversight Committee and the Task Force, transmitted legislative interrogatories to The MITRE Corporation on May 22, 2026 and had previously sent a records demand to MIT Lincoln Laboratory concerning the Beacon Hill Study and the recording 'AF-ATIC-FILM, 03/52'. Source: https://burlison.house.gov/media/press-releases/burlison-presses-mitre-answers-uap-records-ffrdc-accountability-and-compliance
- **Tier 1 documented.** Representative Nancy Mace stated at the same hearing: 'only certain people know the name of the program, and if you do not know it, you cannot get the information.' Source: CHRG-119hhrg61718, 9 September 2025
- **Tier 1 documented.** Section 1687(b) of Public Law 118-31 (Dec. 22, 2023, 137 Stat. 621) provides that independent research and development funding relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena 'shall not be allowable as indirect expenses' for covered contracts under DoD Instruction 3204.01 unless such material and information is made available to the appropriate congressional committees and congressional leadership. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=%28title%3A50+section%3A3373+edition%3Aprelim%29 (statutory note to 50 U.S.C. 3373)
- **Tier 1 documented.** The 26 July 2023 House Oversight hearing took testimony from David Grusch, David Fravor and Ryan Graves before the Subcommittee on National Security, the Border, and Foreign Affairs in 2154 Rayburn. Source: House Oversight Committee hearing notice and transcript CHRG-118hhrg53022
- **Tier 1 documented.** The FY2024 National Defense Authorization Act, Public Law 118-31, sections 1841-1843, requires NARA to establish the 'Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Records Collection' and required each federal agency to review, identify and organize each UAP record in its custody for disclosure and transmission to the National Archives by October 20, 2024. Source: https://www.archives.gov/records-mgmt/uap-guidance (NARA guidance, updated May 8, 2024)
- **Tier 1 documented.** The July 26, 2023 UAP hearing was held by the House Oversight Subcommittee on National Security, the Border, and Foreign Affairs, chaired by Rep. Glenn Grothman with Rep. Robert Garcia as ranking member, and was described by witness Ryan Graves as 'the first public hearing on UAP to hear from military pilots and whistleblowers.' Source: Ryan Graves, written testimony, House Committee on Oversight and Accountability, July 26, 2023; https://oversight.house.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/07/Ryan-HOC-Testimony.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The statutory scope of the UAP Records Collection is 'copies of all Government, Government-provided, or Government-funded records relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena, technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence (or equivalent subjects by any other name with the specific and sole exclusion of temporarily non-attributed objects).' Source: https://www.archives.gov/records-mgmt/uap-guidance
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** In May 2026 the administration began posting declassified interagency UAP files as part of President Trump's disclosure initiative, with the first tranche published on war.gov/ufo, an executive-branch release running parallel to, rather than through, the congressional records process. Source: https://defensescoop.com/2026/05/14/uap-trump-first-pursue-ufo-file-drop/
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** On September 9, 2025 the Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets held a hearing on UAP transparency and whistleblower protection with witnesses Jeffrey Nuccetelli (U.S. Air Force veteran), Chief Alexandro Wiggins, journalist George Knapp, and Dylan Borland (U.S. Air Force veteran). Source: https://defensescoop.com/2025/09/09/military-whistleblowers-share-new-evidence-alleged-uap-ufo-hearing/
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The Safe Airspace for Americans Act, H.R. 6967 (118th Congress), introduced by the Chairman and Ranking Member of the House Oversight National Security Subcommittee, would allow civilian pilots to report UAP through NASA's Aviation Safety Reporting System, provide protection against retaliation, and make all UAP reports publicly available. Source: https://www.congress.gov/bill/118th-congress/house-bill/6967/text (bill text; endorsement noted at https://www.safeaerospace.org/)

## Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency

Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1958-02-07; axes C0 A2 D1; 9 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: Independent R&D agency created in response to Sputnik; contributor to the 2021 UAP assessment.

### What it is

The Advanced Research Projects Agency, established in the Office of the Secretary of Defense by DoD Directive 5105.15 of 7 February 1958 under the authority of the National Security Act of 1947 as amended and Reorganization Plan No. 6 of 1953, with its Director reporting to the Secretary of Defense. Its first Director was Roy W. Johnson.

### Why it is implicated

DARPA is the institutional counterfactual against which the legacy programme allegation is measured: it is what an American breakthrough-technology effort looks like when it is run in the open, with named programme managers on fixed terms, published solicitations and a public budget. Until this pass the node argued that counterfactual while carrying no founding record at all.

### The longer account

The comparison only works if the agency's own paper is on the board, and now it is. The founding instrument is reproduced in full inside the agency's own commissioned history, and two things in it matter. The establishment clause puts ARPA inside the Office of the Secretary of Defense rather than inside a service, and the mandate is written open-ended by design: the Agency is responsible for such advanced projects in the field of research and development as the Secretary of Defense shall from time to time designate, by individual project or by category. That is a charter that can absorb anything the Secretary points at, which is exactly the shape the legacy allegation describes, and it was published. The second correction is to the officer. The CIA's own chronology, an independent record, states that on 7 February 1958 ARPA was established in DoD with Roy W. Johnson of General Electric named director; Herbert York was the first Chief Scientist and was later elevated to first Director of Defense Research and Engineering. The podcast corpus reverses those two, and no York node is built: the correction belongs here, on the agency.

Roles the board assigns it: research, funding.

### Open questions on this node

- The issuing office's own copy of DoD Directive 5105.15 was not opened; esd.whs.mil returned HTTP 403 on every path tried, and the directive's text here comes from the ARPA-commissioned history's reproduction of it.
- Whether DARPA's role among the contributing elements to ODNI's June 2021 Preliminary Assessment extended beyond the developmental-programs explanatory category.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 246 of 1159, score 42). 9 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C0/A2/D1.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** DARPA is named among the contributing elements to ODNI's 25 June 2021 Preliminary Assessment on UAP. Source: ODNI Preliminary Assessment, 25 June 2021
- **Tier 1 documented.** DARPA states it was 'Created in response to the launch of Sputnik in 1957' and is 'an independent research and development agency within the U.S. Department of War (DOW)', with six technical offices. Source: DARPA, About, retrieved 12 August 2026
- **Tier 1 documented.** DARPA's published budget figures as of 2026 are a President's FY2027 request of $5.039 billion and an FY2026 enacted level of $4.322 billion. Source: DARPA, About, retrieved 12 August 2026
- **Tier 1 documented.** The agency has a founding instrument and a first Director, and this node has carried neither. DoD Directive 5105.15 of 7 February 1958, reproduced in full in the agency's own commissioned history, reads: 'In accordance with the provisions of the National Security Act of 1947, as amended, and Reorganization Plan No. 6 of 1953, there is established in the Office of the Secretary of Defense the Department of Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency.' Its mandate is open-ended by design: the Agency is responsible for 'the direction or performance of such advanced projects in the field of research and development as the Secretary of Defense shall, from time to time, designate by individual project or by category', and 'The Director of Advanced Research Projects shall report to the Secretary of Defense.' Source: The Advanced Research Projects Agency, 1958-1974, Richard J. Barber Associates, Inc., prepared for ARPA, Figure II-2, DTIC AD-A154 363, https://archive.org/download/DTIC_ADA154363/DTIC_ADA154363_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The first Director was Roy W. Johnson, not Herbert York. A second agency's record dates the founding and names the officer independently: the CIA Historical Staff Chronology under Jan-Apr 58 reads '7 FEB. Advanced Research Projects Agency (ARPA) established in DOD, Roy W. Johnson of General Electric named director, responsible for US space program, including missiles and anti-missiles.' Herbert York was ARPA's first Chief Scientist and was later elevated to first Director of Defense Research and Engineering. Source: CIA Historical Staff Chronology 1946-65, Volume II 1956-65, June 1970, CIA-RDP85B00803R000200050003-5, https://archive.org/details/cia-readingroom-document-cia-rdp85b00803r000200050003-5

## Senate Armed Services Committee

Senate Armed Services Committee is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1947; axes C1 A3 D1; 9 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: Committee of jurisdiction over the NDAA vehicle.

### What it is

The Committee on Armed Services of the United States Senate, one of the six defense committees named in 10 U.S.C. 119(g), and therefore one of the bodies whose chairman and ranking minority member sit inside the twelve-member group that may be advised of a waived special access program. SASC is also the drafting venue for the annual National Defense Authorization Act provisions that created the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office, defined unidentified anomalous phenomena in statute, and progressively tightened the reporting obligations attached to them.

### Why it is implicated

SASC occupies the structural pinch point: it is simultaneously the committee that is supposed to receive notification of the most closely held programs and the committee that keeps writing new statutes because it apparently is not receiving it. The language it has enacted is an admission. 50 U.S.C. 3373(j) requires AARO's historical record report to itemize any program or activity that was protected by restricted access that has not been explicitly and clearly reported to Congress, and any efforts to obfuscate, manipulate public opinion, hide, or otherwise provide incorrect unclassified or classified information about UAP. Congress does not write that sentence about a subject on which it believes it has been told the truth. Subsection (l)(4) goes further, requiring the AARO Director to hand the SASC and SSCI chair and ranking member an enumeration of every instance in which UAP data was withheld from AARO because of classification restrictions or for any other reason, which is a statutory acknowledgment that data is being withheld from the office created to collect it. And in December 2025 Congress had to amend the statute again to extend the immediate data-availability requirement beyond intelligence community elements to Department of Defense components, meaning that for three years the mandate did not reach the department that owns the special access program system.

### The longer account

Follow the statutory ratchet, because it is a record of what did not work.

In December 2021, section 1683 of the FY2022 NDAA, Public Law 117-81, 135 Stat. 2118, created the office structure and authorities to address what were then called unidentified aerial phenomena. Twelve months later, in December 2022, Public Law 117-263 rewrote the section twice over: section 1673(c) of the NDAA substituted anomalous for aerial throughout and added congressional leadership to the notification list, while section 6802(a) of the Intelligence Authorization Act amended the section generally, standing up the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office proper. The Senate-side drive came from SASC members, principally Senator Kirsten Gillibrand working with Senator Marco Rubio.

What the rewritten section actually demands is the interesting part. AARO's duties include developing procedures to synchronize and standardize the collection, reporting and analysis of UAP incidents, including adverse physiological effects, across the entire Department of Defense and intelligence community. The Secretary of Defense and the Director of National Intelligence must jointly designate an official responsible for ensuring the expertise, authorities, accesses, data, systems, platforms and capabilities exist for rapid response to and support for field investigations, and must designate one or more line organizations primarily responsible for scientific, technical and operational analysis of data, including with respect to the testing of materials, medical studies, and development of theoretical models. Congress wrote materials testing and medical studies into the statute. It also required that each designated line organization have authority to draw on the special expertise of persons outside the federal government with appropriate security clearances, and it listed the Department of Energy among the elements that may detail personnel to be physically located at the office.

The historical record report in subsection (j) is the closest thing to a congressional charge sheet in U.S. law. It requires a written report to the defense committees, the intelligence committees and congressional leadership covering the period beginning January 1, 1945, compiling the key historical record of intelligence community involvement with UAP, and specifically itemizing three things: any restricted-access program or activity not explicitly and clearly reported to Congress; successful or unsuccessful efforts to identify and track UAP; and any efforts to obfuscate, manipulate public opinion, hide, or otherwise provide incorrect information, classified or unclassified. The Archivist of the United States is directed to make NARA holdings, including classified holdings, available to AARO for that purpose. Congress then attached an auditor: section 6803 of Public Law 117-263 required the Comptroller General to identify cleared GAO personnel to audit the historical record report process, to conduct work on site as appropriate, and to brief the intelligence committees, the defense committees and congressional leadership semiannually on AARO's progress and its compliance with legislative requirements. That requirement was extended by Public Law 118-159 section 6802 in December 2024.

The FY2024 NDAA added the money lever. Section 1687(b) of Public Law 118-31 made independent research and development funding relating to UAP unallowable as an indirect expense on contracts covered by DoD Instruction 3204.01 unless the material is made available to the congressional defense committees, both intelligence committees, and the majority and minority leaders of the Senate and the Speaker and minority leader of the House. This is the appropriations-side answer to the classification-side problem, and its existence implies a belief that contractors were recovering UAP-related costs through overhead.

The FY2026 NDAA, Public Law 119-60, enacted December 18, 2025, shows what still was not working. Section 1671 added a new subsection (l)(5) requiring every semiannual briefing to include details on any UAP intercepts conducted by the North American Aerospace Defense Command or United States Northern Command, including the number, location and nature of the intercepts and a description of the procedures and protocols followed, including any data collected or analyzed during such intercepts, and requiring the AARO Director to inform Congress of any failure by NORAD or NORTHCOM to provide timely information. Section 1671(b) reached backward, requiring the first briefing after enactment to cover intercepts not previously reported going back to January 1, 2004. Section 1673(a) gave the AARO Director 180 days to produce an accounting of the security classification guides that apply to information used for UAP reports and investigations, with discretionary authority to issue a consolidated security classification matrix. Section 1672(b) rewrote the data-availability clause so that the immediate-production requirement now binds each element of the intelligence community and component of the Department of Defense with UAP data, where the prior text bound only intelligence community elements.

That last amendment is the single most damning line in the sequence. From December 2022 to December 2025, the statutory command to hand UAP data over immediately did not reach Department of Defense components as such. The department that operates the special access program system, that houses DoD SAPCO, and that owns the classification guides sat outside the mandate for three years, and it took an act of Congress in December 2025 to close it.

What SASC has not delivered is the enforcement architecture. The UAP Disclosure Act, offered by Senators Chuck Schumer and Mike Rounds as an NDAA amendment and modeled on the JFK Assassination Records Collection Act, would have created a nine-member presidentially appointed civilian Review Board with subpoena power and would have asserted federal eminent domain over recovered technologies of unknown origin held by private entities. The Review Board and the eminent domain provision were stripped in conference in FY2024, and the Act was excluded again from the FY2026 NDAA. What survived was a records collection with no independent adjudicator. The one provision that would have reached into contractor custody, eminent domain over recovered technologies of unknown origin, is the one that has never made it into law.

### What it has denied

SASC issues no denials of its own; it is the body to which denials are owed. The relevant posture is the executive branch's, restated through 2026, that government officials have not uncovered evidence verifying any UAP case involving extraterrestrial activity or technology. That position is worth dating against the committee's own enacted text, which since December 2022 has required a formal itemization of restricted-access programs never reported to Congress and of efforts to hide or provide incorrect information about UAP.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, funding, concealment, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Has any SASC chair or ranking member ever received a waived-SAP notification touching UAP, and is there any record of it in the DoD SAPCO congressional-disclosure logs required by DoDD 5205.07?
- What did the subsection (l)(4) enumerations of withheld data actually contain, and how many instances have been reported to date?
- Why did the immediate data-availability mandate exclude Department of Defense components from December 2022 until December 2025, and who benefited from that gap?
- Whether AARO's historical record report, covering January 1, 1945 forward, has itemized any restricted-access program not previously reported to Congress
- What GAO's semiannual audit briefings on AARO's compliance have found, and whether any unclassified version exists
- Why the eminent domain provision over recovered technologies of unknown origin has been removed from every enacted NDAA since 2023, and at whose insistence
- Whether the section 1673(a) accounting of UAP security classification guides was delivered within the 180-day deadline in June 2026

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 433 of 1159, score 33). 9 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A3/D1.

## RAND Corporation

RAND Corporation is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1946; axes C1 A3 D2; 8 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: Nonprofit operator of four FFRDCs (Project AIR FORCE, Arroyo Center, National Defense Research Institute, HSOAC); Santa Monica, CA; est. 1948 out of Project RAND (1946).

### What it is

The RAND Corporation is a nonprofit research institution created by the U.S. Army Air Forces in 1946 as Project RAND, operating under contract to the Douglas Aircraft Company, and spun out as an independent California nonprofit in 1948 with initial capital from the Ford Foundation. It operates federally funded research and development centers for the Department of Defense, including Project AIR FORCE, the National Defense Research Institute and the Army Research Division, and has published on unidentified aerial phenomena in both 1968 and 2023.

### Why it is implicated

RAND is the original defense-contractor-adjacent think tank, born inside an airframe manufacturer and chartered to think about things the Air Force could not think about in uniform. It sat at the center of the 1946-1968 window in which any recovery and exploitation effort would have needed exactly RAND's product: threat framing, capability assessment, and analytic cover. Its 1968 internal draft "UFOs: What To Do?" states flatly that the phenomenon is "unambiguously extraordinary and clearly inexplicable in modern terms", a judgment produced inside the Air Force's own analytic arm during the same period the Condon Committee was preparing to declare the subject scientifically barren. RAND stamped that document for internal use only and reportedly refused to acknowledge its existence for thirty-two years. Fifty-five years later, RAND published an OSD-sponsored study whose central finding is that public UAP reports cluster around military operations areas, analytically useful, and also the most institutionally comfortable finding available, since it relocates the phenomenon inside U.S. military activity without ever addressing recovery or custody.

### The longer account

Project RAND was created in 1946 under contract between the Army Air Forces and the Douglas Aircraft Company, expressly to retain the civilian scientific talent that had won the war. Its first assignment, delivered on a three-week deadline, was the design of a world-circling spaceship; the resulting "Preliminary Design of an Experimental World-Circling Spaceship" was released in May 1946. The organization incorporated as a California nonprofit on 14 May 1948, and on 1 November 1948 the Project RAND contract was formally transferred from Douglas Aircraft to the RAND Corporation, with the Ford Foundation supplying the initial capital for the spin-off.

That founding sequence is the point. RAND was a defense contractor's internal analysis shop that became a national institution by changing its corporate wrapper without changing its customer. The July 1947 Roswell events fall squarely inside the Douglas-contract period; the FBI teletype GAO later recovered records that the recovered disc and balloon were "being sent to Wright Field... for examination", Wright Field being Air Materiel Command, the same enterprise for which Project RAND was doing its earliest work on exotic aerospace concepts. GAO in 1995 could find no Air Materiel Command records from 1947-1950 mentioning the crash or any examination of debris.

RAND's documented engagement with the phenomenon comes in two widely separated bursts.

The first is DRU-1571, "UFOs: What To Do?", by George Kocher, 1968, forty-three pages. RAND's own abstract for the document is unusually direct: "Since World War II, however, there seems to have been a drastic increase in the number of sightings. We have enough data, both visual and photographic, on some of these sightings to know that the phenomenon is unambiguously extraordinary and clearly inexplicable in modern terms." Kocher examines UFO brightness, size and maneuvers, discusses the frequency and location of sightings, and closes by proposing standardized reporting so that times and locations of appearances may be anticipated and objective data obtained. He recommended going beyond a national collection to worldwide data with intensive investigation of the most interesting reports. The document is dated 27 November 1968 and was stamped for RAND use only, with instructions not to quote or cite externally; researchers report that RAND did not acknowledge the paper's existence and authenticity until 2000. RAND now files it in a series it describes as unrestricted drafts that "were not usually formally edited or peer reviewed", a retrospective classification that softens a document written by its own staff during the exact months the Air Force was preparing to shut down Project Blue Book on the strength of the Condon report.

The timing is the finding. In late 1968 the Air Force's analytic contractor produced an internal paper saying the phenomenon was real, extraordinary and inexplicable, and recommending a global standardized collection architecture. In January 1969 the Condon report was released and Blue Book was terminated. The Kocher draft was suppressed for a generation.

The second burst is 2023. RAND published "Not the X-Files: Mapping Public Reports of Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Across America," report number RR-A2475-1, by Marek N. Posard, Ashley Gromis and Mary Lee. The research was sponsored by the Office of the Secretary of Defense and conducted within the Acquisition and Technology Policy Program of the RAND National Security Research Division. The authors analyzed 101,151 public UAP reports across 12,783 U.S. Census Bureau locations. The headline result is that reports cluster near Military Operations Areas, designated blocks of airspace for military training and maneuvering, not necessarily near airbases. RAND completed the report in May 2023, sent it to the Department of Defense for review, and published it on 25 July 2023, the day before the House Oversight Committee's UAP hearing at which David Grusch testified.

For an analyst, the 2023 study is a two-edged artifact. Its finding is genuinely useful: whatever is being reported, it is being reported disproportionately where the military flies. That is consistent with misidentification of military activity, and it is equally consistent with the phenomenon exhibiting interest in military activity, the correlation does not discriminate. What the study does not do, and was not scoped to do, is ask a single question about recovery, custody, materials, or program structure. An OSD-sponsored study, reviewed by the Department before release, published the day before the most consequential UAP hearing in fifty years, framing the entire subject as a geography-of-misidentification problem, is exactly the deliverable a concealment posture would commission. That does not make the analysis wrong. It makes the timing and the scoping evidence about the sponsor.

RAND's structural position remains what it was in 1946: an FFRDC operator with continuous classified access across Project AIR FORCE, the National Defense Research Institute and the Army Research Division, publishing an unclassified fraction of its output and disclaiming that its publications "do not necessarily reflect the opinions of its research clients and sponsors." Between 1968 and 2023 the public record shows nothing. Fifty-five years of an FFRDC's silence on a subject its own analyst called unambiguously extraordinary is itself a data point.

### What it has denied

RAND has never publicly denied anything about UAP; its posture is non-engagement punctuated by two publications fifty-five years apart. The relevant artifact is the withholding: a 1968 internal paper by a RAND analyst declaring the phenomenon unambiguously extraordinary was stamped for internal use only and, per researchers who pursued it, was not acknowledged by RAND as authentic until 2000. The 2023 OSD-sponsored study restores RAND to the subject on terms that frame it entirely as a misidentification-geography problem and never touch recovery, materials or program structure.

Roles the board assigns it: research, concealment, funding.

### Open questions on this node

- What classified RAND work on unidentified aerial objects exists between 1946 and 1968, and does any of it sit in the Project RAND contract files at Air Force History Support Office or NARA?
- Who inside RAND commissioned or approved the Kocher draft, who was on distribution, and why was it restricted to internal use?
- What explains RAND's reported thirty-two-year refusal to acknowledge DRU-1571, and who made that decision?
- Did Project RAND produce any product for Air Materiel Command relating to recovered materials or unconventional aerospace objects in 1947-1950?
- Did the Office of the Secretary of Defense scope the 2023 study to exclude recovery, custody and materials questions, and does the sponsor correspondence survive?
- What did DoD's pre-publication review of RR-A2475-1 change, and is a pre-review draft obtainable?
- Has RAND performed any classified UAP-related work for AARO, ODNI or OSD since 2020 under Project AIR FORCE or NDRI?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 147 of 1159, score 53). 8 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A3/D2.

## Institute of Field Physics (UNC Chapel Hill)

Institute of Field Physics (UNC Chapel Hill) is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1955-09-07; axes C2 A1 D1; 7 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: Privately endowed gravitation-research institute inside the UNC physics department, incorporated 7 September 1955.

### What it is

A privately endowed gravitation-research institute placed inside the physics department at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, incorporated 7 September 1955, directed by Bryce and Cecile DeWitt and funded principally by the Winston-Salem industrialist Agnew Bahnson. Its inaugural event was the January 1957 Conference on the Role of Gravitation in Physics, now known as GR1.

### Why it is implicated

It is where the aerospace money and the academic gravity work meet on the same page: George Trimble and the Glenn L. Martin Company bought a Founder's Membership for $5,000 while the company was separately standing up RIAS, whose first researcher was Louis Witten. And it is the body that wrote its own anti-gravity disclaimer into every publicity statement, which is a documented instance of an institution managing the reputational risk this board maps.

### The longer account

Two records opened in this corpus refine the node rather than extend it, and both refinements cut against an easy reading. The first is sponsorship. The conference report's title page reads that the meeting was held under the sponsorship of the International Union of Pure and Applied Physics, with financial support from UNESCO, the National Science Foundation, the Wright Air Development Center of the US Air Force, and the Office of Ordnance Research of the US Army. IUPAP sponsored it; four bodies paid for it; and the Foreword says the report exists because it was a necessary requirement to obtain conference funds. The Air Force's imprint is on the cover because the report was the condition of the money, not because the Air Force convened the meeting. The second is naming and dating. The conference title page says the Institute was established in 1956 in the Department of Physics, while the certificate of incorporation is dated 7 September 1955: two different acts, which a node should not collapse. And Talbert's Herald Tribune series of 20 November 1955 shows the press knew it at its founding as the Institute of Pure Physics, approved by the trustees that month with Gordon Gray's approval. Four of the forty-six conference participants were serving in US military research offices, which is the fact worth carrying: not that the Air Force ran it, but that it was in the room.

Roles the board assigns it: research, funding, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- What the change of name from Institute of Pure Physics to Institute of Field Physics turned on, which no opened record states.
- Whether the Wright Air Development Center's financial support carried any condition beyond the report.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 154 of 1159, score 52). 7 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C2/A1/D1.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Air Force money and Air Force logistics reached it through a named laboratory: Joshua Goldberg, 'in charge of aspects of the Air Force's funding of general relativity, in the "modern physics research branch" (based at the Aeronautical Research Laboratory at Wright Patterson Air Force Base)', gave a $5,000 grant and secured Military Air Transport Service flights for conference participants from eleven nations. Source: History of the Institute of Field Physics (1955-1959), Section II, https://physics.unc.edu/department-history/section-ii/
- **Tier 1 documented.** Every piece of the Institute's publicity was to carry a disclaimer, written into its design by John Wheeler. The 'Protection Clause' reads in part: 'The work in field physics and gravitation theory carried on at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, and financed by the Institute of Field Physics, as fundraising agency, has no connection with so-called "anti-gravity research" of whatever kind and for whatever purposes.' Source: History of the Institute of Field Physics (1955-1959), Section II, https://physics.unc.edu/department-history/section-ii/
- **Tier 1 documented.** Martin money reached the Institute directly: Trimble and the Glenn Martin Company 'purchase[d] a "Founder's Membership" for the Institute of Field Physics, for the considerable sum of $5000, they also offered their support to solicit further funding'. The same source records that Louis Witten 'was the first researcher hired by RIAS, of the Glenn Martin Company', and identifies the Glenn L. Martin Company as 'now Lockheed-Martin'. Source: History of the Institute of Field Physics (1955-1959), Section II, https://physics.unc.edu/department-history/section-ii/
- **Tier 1 documented.** The 1957 Chapel Hill conference was the Institute's inaugural event: 'In January 1957, a group of physicists from several countries met at the University of North Carolina to discuss the role of gravitation in physics ... The Chapel Hill conference also marked the establishment of the Institute of Field Physics, directed by Bryce and Cecile DeWitt. The conference was the inaugural conference of this institute.' Source: The Role of Gravitation in Physics: Report from the 1957 Chapel Hill Conference, eds. Dean Rickles and Cecile M. DeWitt, Edition Open Sources, Sources 5, 2011, https://edition-open-sources.org/sources/5/index.html
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Institute was incorporated and sited deliberately inside a university: 'The IOFP received its certificate of incorporation on September 7, 1955.' 'The plan of the IOFP was to house an institute within an academic institution, so as to avoid the conflict that physicists felt working in an industrial setting. The chosen location was the physics department at the University of North Carolina, Chapel Hill.' Source: History of the Institute of Field Physics (1955-1959), Dean Rickles, UNC Chapel Hill Department of Physics and Astronomy department history, Section II, https://physics.unc.edu/department-history/section-ii/
- **Tier 1 documented.** The January 1957 Chapel Hill conference had one sponsor and four funders, and the Air Force was not the sponsor. The report's title page reads: 'UNDER THE SPONSORSHIP OF THE International Union of Pure and Applied Physics, with financial support from UNESCO / National Science Foundation / Wright Air Development Center, U.S. Air Force / Office of Ordnance Research, U.S. Army'. The Foreword gives the reason the report exists at all: 'The present report was undertaken as a necessary requirement to obtain conference funds.' WADC's imprint is on the cover because the report was the condition of the money, not because the Air Force convened the meeting. Source: The Role of Gravitation in Physics: Report from the 1957 Chapel Hill Conference, WADC Technical Report 57-216, title page and Foreword by Cecile M. DeWitt, 15 March 1957, https://edition-open-sources.org/sources/5/5/index.html
- **Tier 1 documented.** The press knew the Institute under a different name at its founding. Talbert's Herald Tribune series of 20 November 1955 reports: 'A proposal to establish at the University of North Carolina at Chapel Hill, N. C., an Institute of Pure Physics primarily to carry on theoretical research on gravity was approved earlier this month by the university's board of trustees. This had the approval of Dr. Gordon Gray, who has since retired as president of the university.' It names Agnew H. Bahnson jr. as the fundraiser and says the directorship had been offered to Bryce S. DeWitt. Source: Ansel E. Talbert, New York Herald Tribune, 20 November 1955, continuation p. 36, https://archive.org/download/talbert1ny/talbert1ny_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** Two different acts are recorded for the Institute and a node should not collapse them. The conference report's title page states: 'This conference was an activity of the North Carolina Project of the Institute of Field Physics, established in 1956 in the Department of Physics of the University of North Carolina, Chapel Hill.' The certificate of incorporation this node carries is dated 7 September 1955; the establishment inside the department is dated 1956 by the conference's own paper. Source: The Role of Gravitation in Physics: Report from the 1957 Chapel Hill Conference, WADC Technical Report 57-216, title page, https://edition-open-sources.org/sources/5/5/index.html
