# 1970-1999: Post-Blue Book

## 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: Congressional Oversight

10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: Congressional Oversight is a legislation on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1989; axes C0 A3 D3; 16 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: statute.

### What it is

10 U.S.C. § 119, 'Special access programs: congressional oversight,' is the statute that governs how the Department of Defense must tell Congress about its special access programs. Enacted 4 December 1987 as section 1132(a)(1) of Public Law 100-180 and amended in 1990, 1996, 1999 and 2001, it requires an annual report on all SAPs, an annual report on each new SAP with justification, advance notice of classification changes, and, decisively, bars any SAP from being initiated until the defense committees have been notified and thirty days have elapsed. It also grants the Secretary of Defense a case-by-case waiver over the content of those reports.

### Why it is implicated

This is the statute against which the concealment must be measured, and it is the statute a whistleblower report given to Congress in 2024 alleges is being deliberately circumvented. Subsection (f) is categorical: a special access program 'may not be initiated until' the defense committees are notified and thirty days pass. If a legacy retrieval or reverse-engineering effort exists as a DoD SAP and the defense committees were never notified, the illegality is not a matter of interpretation. Subsection (e) is the built-in escape hatch, letting the Secretary strip content from the reports on national-security grounds and narrow disclosure to twelve members. And the reporting obligations in subsections (a) and (b) were terminated outright effective 31 December 2021 by section 1061 of Public Law 114-328, the annual SAP report to Congress simply stopped, in the same window in which the UAP Task Force was being stood up and AARO was being legislated. Michael Shellenberger's sworn testimony that an alleged UAP USAP is White-House-controlled and DoD-executed 'to avoid compliance with Title 10' is a claim about this section specifically.

### The longer account

Section 119 was Congress's answer to the black-budget expansion of the 1980s. Enacted in the FY1988 defense authorisation, it imposed a reporting discipline on a category of program that by design resists reporting. Read as a whole, the section is a fairly aggressive assertion of legislative prerogative, and its subsequent amendment history shows Congress tightening it further: in 1990 Congress added subsection (f), the thirty-day pre-initiation notification bar, effective 1 October 1991, and rewrote subsection (c) to require fourteen days' advance notice of classification changes rather than thirty days' notice after the fact.

The reporting architecture has four parts. Under subsection (a), by 1 March each year the Secretary of Defense must submit to the defense committees a report on special access programs setting out the total amount requested for SAPs in the President's budget and, for each SAP in that budget, a brief description, a brief discussion of major milestones, the actual cost for every prior fiscal year of the program, and the estimated total cost plus estimates for the current year, the budget year, and each of the four succeeding years. Under subsection (b), by 1 February each year the Secretary must report every new SAP with notice of the designation, justification for it, the current estimate of total program cost, and, a provision with real analytic teeth, 'an identification of existing programs or technologies that are similar to the technology, or that have a mission similar to the mission, of the program that is the subject of the notice.' That clause exists to stop the Department from hiding duplicate or successor programs under new names. Under subsection (c), any planned classification change or public declassification announcement requires a report to the defense committees not less than fourteen days beforehand, unless exceptional circumstances make that impossible.

Subsection (e) is the pressure valve. The Secretary 'may waive any requirement under subsection (a), (b), or (c) that certain information be included in a report' if inclusion 'would adversely affect the national security,' with the waiver made on a case-by-case basis. When a waiver is used, the withheld information and the justification go 'jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees', that is, to twelve members across six bodies, and to nobody else. This is the statutory basis of what practitioners call a waived SAP. The implementing directive confirms the practice: access to waived SAPs is limited to the chairs, ranking minority members and staff directors of the defense and intelligence committees unless the Secretary or Deputy Secretary approves otherwise.

Subsection (f) is the hardest edge in the statute and the one most relevant to this board: 'A special access program may not be initiated until, (1) the defense committees are notified of the program; and (2) a period of 30 days elapses after such notification is received.' There is no waiver of subsection (f) in subsection (e), the waiver reaches only (a), (b) and (c). Congress can, in principle, be denied the content of a report about a SAP. It cannot lawfully be denied notice that a DoD SAP is being initiated.

Which makes the terminating note in the current code text the most consequential single fact in this dossier. Under the heading 'Termination of Reporting Requirements,' the statute records: 'For termination, effective Dec. 31, 2021, of provisions in subsecs. (a) and (b) of this section requiring submittal of annual report to Congress, see section 1061 of Pub. L. 114-328.' The annual comprehensive SAP report and the annual new-SAP report both lapsed at the end of 2021, under a sunset written into the FY2017 NDAA. The pre-initiation notification in (f) survives. The routine, systematic, year-over-year visibility does not. Any analyst working this board should note the coincidence of timing: the UAP Task Force was established in August 2020, the ODNI preliminary assessment landed in June 2021, AARO was legislated in December 2021 and stood up in 2022, and the statutory obligation to give Congress an annual accounting of every DoD special access program expired on 31 December 2021.

The implementing machinery underneath the statute is set out in DoD Directive 5205.07, reissued effective 12 September 2024 and approved by Deputy Secretary of Defense Kathleen H. Hicks. It establishes the Special Access Program Oversight Committee chaired by the Deputy Secretary, a Senior Review Group, a Senior Working Group, and the DoD Special Access Program Central Office whose Director reports directly to the Deputy Secretary and is the 'DoD primary point of contact for SAP matters with agencies of the Executive Branch, the Judicial Branch, and the Legislative Branch.' Two provisions in it bear directly on the concealment argument. First, establishment of new SAP umbrellas and compartments is 'by exception' and requires Secretary or Deputy Secretary approval. Second, and more starkly: 'No DoD contractor entity, contractor employee, contractor representative, or consultant will provide SAP material to any members of Congress or their staff without Director, DoD SAPCO approval.' The contractor layer, precisely where a legacy program would sit, is prohibited by directive from talking to Congress about SAP material without permission from an office reporting to the Deputy Secretary of Defense. And the same directive designates USD(I&S), the office housing AARO, as the primary interface with ODNI for sharing compartmented capabilities into and out of DoD SAPs.

Witnesses have made the statutory argument directly. Tim Gallaudet told House Oversight in November 2024 that 'even the most sensitive Special Access Programs must be reported the Gang of Eight' and that 'any and all past failures by DoD and IC components to report UAP programs and activities are not only illegal but would have undermined Congress by preventing it from exercising its legislative and oversight responsibilities.' Shellenberger's source described the alleged workaround with unusual precision: the White House directs the Secretary of Defense to run the program, so it is executed by DoD but controlled by the White House. 'But that's the loophole.' Whether such a structure escapes subsection (f) turns on whether it is a special access program 'of the Department of Defense', a question no court and no committee has resolved on the public record.

### What it has denied

There is no denial attached to the statute itself. The relevant absence is that no DoD component has ever publicly identified a UAP-related special access program in any section 119 report, and the annual reporting obligations that would have carried such an identification lapsed on 31 December 2021. The Department's only categorical statement in this area is Sue Gough's denial that any SAP named IMMACULATE CONSTELLATION appears in its records.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, concealment, funding.

### Open questions on this node

- Has the section 119(e) waiver authority ever been exercised for a UAP-related program, and do the twelve recipients' offices hold the resulting notifications?
- Was the 31 December 2021 termination of the subsection (a) and (b) annual reports replaced by any successor reporting vehicle, or is there now no periodic comprehensive SAP accounting to Congress at all?
- Is a program directed by the White House and executed by the Secretary of Defense a 'special access program of the Department of Defense' for purposes of subsection (f)? No court or committee has resolved this on the public record.
- Who is the current Director of the DoD Special Access Program Central Office, and what records does that office hold of SAP material provided to Congress?
- Does any section 119 report ever filed identify a program with a UAP, materials-exploitation or recovered-technology mission, and would the subsection (b)(2)(B) similar-technology identification requirement have forced such a disclosure?
- How does section 119 interact with intelligence-community programs funded outside the DoD budget, where the reporting path runs to the intelligence committees rather than the defense committees?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 33 of 1159, score 92). 19 connections, 3 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C0/A3/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119 makes no cross-reference to 50 U.S.C. 3348. The traffic runs one way: 3348 names 119 in order to exclude the Department of Defense from itself, and 119 does not name 3348 at all. The only external statute 119 cites is 31 U.S.C. 1105, on the President's budget submission. Source: Full text of 10 U.S.C. 119, OLRC prelim, read 22 August 2026, https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(a)(1) requires that 'not later than March 1 of each year, the Secretary of Defense shall submit to the defense committees a report on special access programs.' Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(a)(1): 'Not later than March 1 of each year, the Secretary of Defense shall submit to the defense committees a report on special access programs.' Source: 10 U.S.C. 119(a)(1)
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(a)(1): 'Not later than March 1 of each year, the Secretary of Defense shall submit to the defense committees a report on special access programs.' Subsection (a)(2) requires, for each SAP in the budget, a brief description, major milestones, actual cost for each prior fiscal year, and estimated total cost plus five out-years. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-2023-title10/html/USCODE-2023-title10-subtitleA-partI-chap2-sec119.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(a)(2)(B) requires that for each special access program in the budget the report include a brief description, a brief discussion of major milestones, the actual cost for each prior fiscal year of the program, and the estimated total cost plus estimates for the current fiscal year, the budget year, and each of the four succeeding fiscal years. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(b) requires an annual report, not later than February 1, on each new special access program providing notice of the designation, justification for it, the current estimate of total program cost, and 'an identification of existing programs or technologies that are similar to the technology, or that have a mission similar to the mission, of the program that is the subject of the notice.' Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(b)(1): 'Not later than February 1 of each year, the Secretary of Defense shall submit to the defense committees a report that, with respect to each new special access program, provides--(A) notice of the designation of the program as a special access program; and (B) justification for such designation.' (b)(2) adds current total program cost estimate and 'an identification of existing programs or technologies that are similar to the technology, or that have a mission similar to the mission, of the program that is the subject of the notice.' Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-2023-title10/html/USCODE-2023-title10-subtitleA-partI-chap2-sec119.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(b)(3) defines 'new special access program' as 'a special access program that has not previously been covered in a notice and justification under this subsection.' Source: 10 U.S.C. 119(b)(3)
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(c) requires the Secretary of Defense to report to the defense committees any planned change in the classification of a DoD special access program, or any planned declassification and public announcement, not less than 14 days beforehand, with a later-notice exception for 'exceptional circumstances' that must be explained in the report. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(c)(2): a report on a planned classification change or public declassification of a DoD SAP 'shall be submitted not less than 14 days before the date on which the proposed change or public announcement is to occur' -- but (c)(3) lets the Secretary submit it 'at any time before' if 'because of exceptional circumstances' the 14 days cannot be met, with an explanation. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-2023-title10/html/USCODE-2023-title10-subtitleA-partI-chap2-sec119.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(d): 'Whenever there is a modification or termination of the policy and criteria used for designating a program of the Department of Defense as a special access program, the Secretary of Defense shall promptly notify the defense committees of such modification or termination.' This is the hook that makes each reissuance of DoDD 5205.07 a reportable event. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-2023-title10/html/USCODE-2023-title10-subtitleA-partI-chap2-sec119.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(1) permits the Secretary of Defense to waive any requirement under subsections (a), (b) or (c) that certain information be included in a report 'if the Secretary determines that inclusion of that information in the report would adversely affect the national security,' with any such waiver made on a case-by-case basis. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(2) provides that when a waiver is exercised, the withheld information and the justification for the waiver go 'jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees', twelve members in total. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(f) provides that 'a special access program may not be initiated until, (1) the defense committees are notified of the program; and (2) a period of 30 days elapses after such notification is received.' The waiver authority in subsection (e) does not extend to subsection (f). Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(f): 'A special access program may not be initiated until - (1) the defense committees are notified of the program; and (2) a period of 30 days elapses after such notification is received.' Source: 10 U.S.C. 119(f)
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(f): 'A special access program may not be initiated until--(1) the defense committees are notified of the program; and (2) a period of 30 days elapses after such notification is received.' Note that (f) is NOT among the subsections waivable under (e)(1), which reaches only subsections (a), (b), and (c). Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-2023-title10/html/USCODE-2023-title10-subtitleA-partI-chap2-sec119.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(g) defines 'defense committees' as the Committee on Armed Services and the Committee on Appropriations, and the Defense Subcommittee of the Committee on Appropriations, of the Senate; and the Committee on Armed Services and the Committee on Appropriations, and the Subcommittee on Defense of the Committee on Appropriations, of the House. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-2023-title10/html/USCODE-2023-title10-subtitleA-partI-chap2-sec119.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(g) defines 'defense committees' as the Committee on Armed Services, the Committee on Appropriations, and the Defense Subcommittee of the Committee on Appropriations of the Senate, and the corresponding committees and Subcommittee on Defense of the House. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. Sec. 119(e) permits the Secretary of Defense to waive SAP reporting requirements where disclosure would harm national security, providing the information and justification only 'jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees.' Source: 10 U.S.C. Sec. 119, current through the prelim edition of the US Code
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoD Directive 5205.07 paragraph 3.1(d) provides that 'establishment of new SAP umbrellas and compartments will be by exception' and that establishing SAPs to protect new and novel critical program information at any tier 'requires Secretary of Defense or Deputy Secretary of Defense approval.' Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/portals/54/documents/dd/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoD Directive 5205.07 paragraph 3.1(g) provides that 'no DoD contractor entity, contractor employee, contractor representative, or consultant will provide SAP material to any members of Congress or their staff without Director, DoD SAPCO approval.' Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/portals/54/documents/dd/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoD Directive 5205.07, effective 12 September 2024 and approved by Deputy Secretary of Defense Kathleen H. Hicks, establishes the Special Access Program Oversight Committee chaired by the Deputy Secretary of Defense and provides that the Director, DoD SAPCO reports directly to the Deputy Secretary and serves as the 'DoD primary point of contact for SAP matters with agencies of the Executive Branch, the Judicial Branch, and the Legislative Branch, including support to SAP congressional meetings, hearings, and responses to congressional inquiries.' Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/portals/54/documents/dd/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** MATERIAL NEW FINDING - THE REPORTS THE WAIVER ATTACHES TO HAVE LARGELY SUNSET. The Office of the Law Revision Counsel carries a statutory note under 10 U.S.C. 119 headed 'Termination of Reporting Requirements' which reads verbatim: 'For termination, effective Dec. 31, 2021, of provisions in subsecs. (a) and (b) of this section requiring submittal of annual report to Congress, see section 1061 of Pub. L. 114-328, set out as a note under section 111 of this title.' The annual special access program report under 119(a) and the new-program notice and justification report under 119(b) therefore terminated as statutory requirements on 31 December 2021. After that date the only report to which the 119(e)(1) waiver can attach is the 119(c) report on a planned classification change or public declassification announcement. This reshapes the board's question: for activity after 31 December 2021 there may be no 119(a) or 119(b) report in existence to waive information out of, which would make the absence of any 119(e)(2) delivery unsurprising rather than suspicious for that window - while for the period 1988 through 2021 the reports were mandatory and the waiver machinery was live. Source: Office of the Law Revision Counsel, 10 U.S.C. 119, 'Special access programs: congressional oversight' (text in effect on 20 August 2026) - https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented, allegation shaped wording as stored.** Michael Shellenberger testified on 13 November 2024 that a source told him an alleged UAP Unacknowledged Special Access Program 'is controlled by the White House and executed and administrated by the DOD to avoid compliance with Title 10 of the United States Code,' adding 'the White House directed the SECDEF to run it. But that's the loophole.' Source: https://oversight.house.gov/wp-content/uploads/2024/11/Written-Testimony-Shellenberger.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Neither Sec. 1687 nor Sec. 7343 of Pub. L. 118-31 is set out as a statutory note under 10 U.S.C. 119. The notes carried under Section 119 are limited to the 1990 effective-date note, the termination-of-reporting note, and the 1987 initial-reports note. Whatever the FY2024 NDAA did to UAP funding, it did not attach itself to the Section 119 oversight architecture in the U.S. Code. Source: Office of the Law Revision Counsel, 10 U.S.C. 119, 'Special access programs: congressional oversight' (text in effect on 20 August 2026) - https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim

A further 14 claims sit on this node on the board and are not reproduced here.

### Its strongest connections

- 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: Congressional Oversight runs out to **United States Department of Defense (secondary designation: Department of War)** (institution, board tier A), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Imposes the entire SAP reporting duty on the Secretary of Defense. Section 119 runs to 'the Secretary of Defense' alone; no subordinate official, program manager or contractor is an obligor under it. The link reads: created by 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(2) and defined by 119(g). Section 119 was added by Pub. L. 100-180 div. A title XI sec. 1132(a)(1), 4 December 1987. Subsection (e)(2) directs waived-SAP information and the waiver justification jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each defense committee; subsection (g) supplies the six-body definition of 'defense committees' used by the section. Sources: 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: congressional oversight, subsections (e)(2) and (g) (OLRC prelim text, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22; 10 U.S.C. 119, Special access programs: congressional oversight (full text, U.S. Code 2023 Edition, GPO), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-2023-title10/html/USCODE-2023-title10-subtitleA-partI-chap2-sec119.htm, dated 2024-01-05.
- 10 U.S.C. 119, Special Access Programs: Congressional Oversight runs out to **Funding Channels** (domain, board tier A), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: the statute that fixes what Congress is told about SAP cost, and subsection (e) which suspends it. Sec. 119(a)-(c) requires the annual SAP report to the four defense committees to state actual historical costs and estimated future costs for each program. Sec. 119(e)(1) lets the Secretary waive any of that on a case-by-case national-security determination, with the information and the justification going instead 'jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees.' The cost visibility this domain measures is created and removed by this one section. Sources: 10 U.S.C. 119 - Special access programs: congressional oversight (Office of the Law Revision Counsel), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-21; 10 U.S.C. 119 (U.S. Code 2023 Edition, GPO), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-2023-title10/html/USCODE-2023-title10-subtitleA-partI-chap2-sec119.htm, dated 2024-01-05.

## National Institute for Discovery Science (NIDS)

National Institute for Discovery Science (NIDS) is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1995 to 2004; axes C2 A2 D2; 12 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: Privately funded research institute, Las Vegas, 1995-2004.

### What it is

The National Institute for Discovery Science was a privately financed research organization based in Las Vegas, Nevada, formed by real-estate developer Robert Bigelow in October 1995 and disbanded in October 2004. It bought the 480-acre Sherman ranch in the Uinta Basin near Fort Duchesne, Utah, the property later known as Skinwalker Ranch, in 1996 and staffed it around the clock with scientists and a veterinarian. It was the direct organizational and personnel predecessor to Bigelow Aerospace Advanced Space Studies, the contractor that executed the Defense Intelligence Agency's AAWSAP contract from 2008.

### Why it is implicated

NIDS is the point at which anomaly research moved into private hands under conditions of contractual secrecy, and it is the documented bridge from that private effort into a DIA-funded government program. Within weeks of buying the ranch Bigelow had the previous owner, Terry Sherman, working for him under a nondisclosure agreement that Sherman told the Las Vegas Sun prevented him from discussing what happened on the property, a private-sector information control instrument doing exactly the work a security classification would do, without any of the accountability. NIDS drew its personnel directly from the national security research establishment: John B. Alexander, the former director of non-lethal weapons testing at Los Alamos National Laboratory, was the institute's public voice from its Las Vegas offices in 1996. And the continuity to government is not inferential: Rice University's published biography of Colm Kelleher states that he led the NIDS team on Skinwalker Ranch from 1996 to 2004 and that "in 2008, Kelleher became deputy administrator of Bigelow Aerospace Advanced Space Studies (BAASS) where he led the day-to-day operations in executing the AAWSAP contract with Defense Intelligence Agency (DIA)." NIDS is where the government's later program acquired its methodology, its site, its database concept and its senior staff.

### The longer account

The sequence is tight and dated. In July 1996 the Sherman family broke years of silence and went public in the Deseret News and on national radio with accounts of strange lights, lights emerging from circular "doorways" that appeared in mid-air, three cows mutilated, several others vanished, unusual soil impressions and circles of flattened grass. Several weeks later Robert Bigelow met the Shermans and negotiated to buy the 480-acre ranch for about $200,000. By 23 October 1996, when the Las Vegas Sun published its account, Bigelow had erected an observation building, moved in a pair of scientists and a veterinarian, and had someone on the property twenty-four hours a day recording anything out of the ordinary. The Shermans relocated fifteen miles away. Terry Sherman stayed on as ranch caretaker, employed by Bigelow, and told the Sun he could no longer discuss the activity because of a nondisclosure agreement Bigelow had him sign. He also said he had been offered more money by a Colorado family and a local hunting club but did not want to put anyone at risk. His summary: "It's just been a bad deal all the way around. All that's really redeeming is that you have some knowledge that a lot of people don't have, for what it's worth."

The institutional framing was scientific and the operating posture was closed. Officially the research was conducted by the National Institute for Discovery Science, which Bigelow had formed the previous October. John B. Alexander, described in the same article as "former director of non-lethal weapons testing at Los Alamos National Laboratories in New Mexico", spoke for the institute and articulated the public mission: "Our approach is to do good, high-quality research using a standard scientific approach and do what we can to get hard data. One of the missions of the institute is to make information widely available." He said results would be published in scientific journals and on the institute's web page. Bigelow declined to be interviewed. Alexander would not provide details of how or why the research was being conducted. Outside researchers, including Chris O'Brien, who had visited the ranch in July before Bigelow's involvement and called it "the most impressive case I've ever personally investigated," objected that "it should be public knowledge."

The cattle-mutilation program was built deliberately. Bigelow met Roosevelt, Utah veterinarian Dan Dennis on 15 October 1996 to ask for assistance performing necropsies on any animals whose deaths or mutilations might be tied to UFO or paranormal phenomena, and planned to write to area ranchers requesting notification of unusual animal deaths. From 1999 NIDS ran a public hotline; over five thousand reports arrived by phone and email, many later attributed to missile test launches and meteors. NIDS's stated research areas were cattle mutilation and black triangle reports. Colm Kelleher, deputy administrator, framed the scope: "We don't study aliens, we study anomalies. They're the same thing in a lot of people's minds, but not in our minds." NIDS disbanded in October 2004.

What matters for this board is what happened next. Kelleher went from NIDS to Prosetta in San Francisco from 2004 to 2008, where he was laboratory director executing Department of Defense contracts to discover drugs against Ebola virus and other viruses of interest to DoD, a four-year interval inside the DoD contracting system. Then, per Rice University's published biography, "in 2008, Kelleher became deputy administrator of Bigelow Aerospace Advanced Space Studies (BAASS) where he led the day-to-day operations in executing the AAWSAP contract with Defense Intelligence Agency (DIA)." Rice lists his affiliation on that page as "Program Manager, Advanced Aerospace System Weapons Application Program (AAWSAP) Contractor, Defense Intelligence Agency (DIA)." From 2012 to 2020 he ran the Environmental Control and Life Support Systems department at Bigelow Aerospace, building life-support systems for expandable spacecraft. He co-authored Hunt for the Skinwalker (2005) with George Knapp and Skinwalkers at the Pentagon (2021) with James Lacatski and Knapp.

The archival record now treats NIDS and BAASS as a continuous object. The finding aid for the Jacques F. Vallee UFO and paranormal phenomena papers at Rice describes the collection as containing "field notes and press documents about related topics such as cattle mutilations, and face-to-face meetings of NIDS and BAASS, as well as various research proposals." Vallee, one of the reported NIDS science advisory board members, is thus the custodian of documentation on the meetings that connect the private institute to the DIA contractor, and that documentation sits in a collection restricted until 2028 and 2031.

The analytic significance of NIDS is not that it proved anything. None of the NIDS work was published in a peer-reviewed journal despite Alexander's 1996 commitment. The significance is structural. NIDS demonstrated that a private individual with sufficient capital could stand up an anomaly-research operation staffed by former national-laboratory and intelligence-community personnel, acquire physical custody of a site of interest, impose nondisclosure obligations on witnesses, collect data outside any government records system, and then, twelve years later, sell that same capability, that same site, that same database concept and that same staff back to the Defense Intelligence Agency under a contract. Whatever NIDS collected between 1996 and 2004 was never subject to FOIA, never subject to congressional oversight, and was never in a federal recordkeeping system. That is a concealment architecture built out of contract law rather than classification, and it is the model the AAWSAP era inherited.

### What it has denied

NIDS did not deny; it declined. Bigelow refused interviews, Alexander refused to explain how or why the research was being conducted, and the previous owner was placed under a nondisclosure agreement within weeks of the sale. Alexander's 1996 public commitment that results would appear in scientific journals was not fulfilled, no NIDS finding was published in a peer-reviewed journal in the institute's nine-year existence.

Roles the board assigns it: research, custody, concealment, funding.

### Open questions on this node

- The full NIDS science advisory board roster, its meeting minutes and its charter have never been published. Secondary accounts name Kit Green as chairman with Hal Puthoff, John B. Alexander, Jacques Vallee, John Schuessler, Edgar Mitchell and Harrison Schmitt among the participants; no primary document confirms this.
- What became of the NIDS data holdings when the institute disbanded in October 2004? Were they transferred to Bigelow Aerospace, to BAASS in 2008, or destroyed?
- The terms and scope of the Sherman nondisclosure agreement have never been disclosed. Is it still in force, and how many other witnesses signed comparable instruments?
- Did any federal agency fund, task or receive NIDS product between 1995 and 2004, before the 2008 DIA contract?
- NIDS ran a hotline receiving over 5,000 reports. Where is that report corpus now, and did any of it enter the AAWSAP database?
- Alexander committed publicly in 1996 to journal publication. Was that commitment abandoned by choice, by the absence of results, or by an internal restriction?
- Vallee's papers document face-to-face meetings between NIDS and BAASS. Who attended, when, and what was transferred? The files are restricted until 2028-2031.
- The AAWSAP solicitation and contract numbers reported in secondary accounts (solicitation HHM402-08-R-0211; contract HHM402-08-C-0072, performance beginning 22 September 2008) have not been verified here against a primary contracting record.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 129 of 1159, score 55). 14 connections, 4 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A2/D2.

## Waived unacknowledged special access program (waived USAP)

Waived unacknowledged special access program (waived USAP) is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1988; axes C3 A3 D3; 11 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: The specific legal category at the centre of every allegation on this board.

### What it is

A waived unacknowledged special access program is the most tightly held category in the Department of Defense classification system. Department of Defense training doctrine defines it in one sentence: 'Waived SAPs are unacknowledged SAPs for which the Secretary of Defense has waived applicable reporting requirements under Section 119, Title 10 U.S. Code.' The waiver authority is 10 U.S.C. 119(e), under which the Secretary may withhold specified information from the statutory reports to Congress if he determines that including it would adversely affect national security, and must then provide the information and the justification for the waiver only to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees.

### Why it is implicated

This is the mechanism. If a legacy recovery and reverse-engineering program exists and has been concealed from Congress, waived SAP status is the lawful instrument by which that concealment is accomplished, and no unlawful act is required to sustain it. The statute permits an entire program's existence, cost, milestones and technology to disappear from the reports Congress wrote to keep itself informed, on the unilateral written determination of a single official. The population read in on the legislative side collapses to twelve members and, per DoD policy, their staff directors. Everyone else in Congress, including every member of both intelligence committees who is not chair or ranking, can hold a Top Secret clearance and still be told nothing exists. The system is designed to produce truthful denials from officials who genuinely do not know, which is the precise pathology alleged across every UAP whistleblower account since 2017.

### The longer account

The architecture has four layers, and each one narrows the audience.

Layer one is the baseline reporting duty. 10 U.S.C. 119, added by Public Law 100-180 section 1132(a)(1) on December 4, 1987, required the Secretary of Defense to submit to the defense committees an annual report on special access programs by March 1 of each year, setting out the total amount requested for SAPs in the President's budget and, for each SAP, a brief description, its major milestones, its actual cost for each prior fiscal year, and its estimated total cost across the current year, the budget year and four succeeding years. Subsection (b) required a separate annual report by February 1 giving notice and justification for each new SAP designation, with the current total program cost estimate and an identification of existing programs or technologies similar in technology or mission to the one being hidden. Subsection (f), added by Public Law 101-510 section 1482(a)(2) in November 1990, added the hard gate: a special access program may not be initiated until the defense committees are notified and thirty days elapse.

Layer two is the waiver. Subsection (e)(1) provides that the Secretary of Defense may waive any requirement under subsection (a), (b), or (c) that certain information be included in a report under that subsection if the Secretary determines that inclusion of that information in the report would adversely affect the national security, with each waiver made on a case-by-case basis. Subsection (e)(2) then routes the withheld information and the justification jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees. Steven Aftergood of the Federation of American Scientists put the practical result plainly: waived SAPs are exempted by statute (10 USC 119e) from normal congressional notification requirements, and in such cases only eight senior members of the congressional defense committees may be advised of the program.

Layer three is DoD policy, which is more restrictive than the statute. Under the pre-2024 SAP policy framework, only the chair, the ranking minority member and the staff directors of the defense and intelligence committees are authorized access to waived DoD SAPs within their committee's oversight role, absent approval by the Secretary or Deputy Secretary. The currently effective directive, DoD Directive 5205.07, reissued September 12, 2024 and approved by Deputy Secretary of Defense Kathleen H. Hicks, adds a provision that is the operational choke point for whistleblowers: no DoD contractor entity, contractor employee, contractor representative, or consultant will provide SAP material to any members of Congress or their staff without Director, DoD SAPCO approval. The people most likely to hold the evidence are contractors, and the directive forbids them from handing it to Congress without permission from the office that runs the SAP system.

Layer four is the quiet erosion of the reporting baseline itself. The U.S. Code note under section 119 records that for termination, effective December 31, 2021, of the provisions in subsections (a) and (b) requiring submittal of an annual report to Congress, see section 1061 of Public Law 114-328. The annual SAP report and the annual new-SAP notification report, the two instruments the 1987 Congress built, were sunset by a general reporting-requirement cull. And the September 12, 2024 reissuance of DoDD 5205.07 does not define waived SAP anywhere in its glossary. It defines SAP, SAP architecture, SAP umbrella, SAP compartment, SAP sub-compartment, Joint Force Integration SAP and Strategic Enabler SAP. The acknowledged, unacknowledged and waived taxonomy that appears in DoD's own training material has been replaced in the public directive by a vocabulary that does not distinguish between programs Congress is told about and programs it is not. Whether that is reform or relabeling, the public governing document no longer names the category.

The rest of the directive maps the custody chain. All DoD SAPs are assigned to an oversight authority drawn from USD(R&E), USD(A&S), USD(P), USD(I&S) and the Director of the DoD SAP Central Office. The Director of DoD SAPCO reports directly to the Deputy Secretary of Defense, holds delegated Top Secret original classification authority for all DoD SAP information, publishes and maintains the security classification guides for every SAP umbrella, accredits congressional SAP facilities, and maintains records of DoD SAP information provided to Congress. Establishment of new SAP umbrellas and compartments is by exception and requires Secretary or Deputy Secretary approval. The senior governing body, the SAP Oversight Committee, is chaired by the Deputy Secretary of Defense. Approximately 75 to 80 per cent of all DoD SAPs are acquisition SAPs, which is the category that would cover a materials exploitation or reverse-engineering effort.

Congress has attacked the funding side rather than the classification side. FY2024 NDAA section 1687(b) makes independent research and development funding relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena unallowable as an indirect expense on covered contracts unless the material and information is made available to the congressional defense committees, both intelligence committees, and the majority and minority leaders of the Senate and the Speaker and minority leader of the House. That is an admission on the face of the statute that Congress believed UAP work was being charged to the taxpayer through contractor overhead and reported to no one. It is also a route around the waiver, because IR&D allowability is an accounting question, not a classification question.

### What it has denied

There is no denial to catalogue, because the Department has never publicly acknowledged any individual waived SAP; acknowledging one would defeat the category. What is on the record instead is a quieter act: the September 12, 2024 reissuance of DoD Directive 5205.07 removed the acknowledged, unacknowledged and waived taxonomy from the public policy document entirely, while DoD's own unclassified training material continues to teach it. The public governing directive no longer names the category by which programs are lawfully hidden from Congress.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, funding, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- How many waived SAPs currently exist, and has that number ever been disclosed even to the twelve?
- Whether the September 12, 2024 removal of the waived-SAP taxonomy from DoDD 5205.07 reflects abolition of the category, its migration into 'Strategic Enabler SAP', or only a change in what the public document says
- What replaced the annual section 119(a) and (b) reports after their December 31, 2021 termination, and whether anything replaced them at all
- Whether any of the twelve members has ever been briefed on a UAP-related waived SAP, and whether the statute permits them to confirm or deny that
- How Alternative Compensatory Control Measures, which sit below SAP thresholds and carry lighter reporting, are being used for UAP-adjacent activity
- Whether the Director, DoD SAPCO's mandated records of DoD SAP information provided to Congress are themselves discoverable, and whether they show any UAP-related entries
- How Department of Energy Restricted Data and Formerly Restricted Data authorities interact with, or supersede, the 10 U.S.C. 119 framework for recovered material

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 93 of 1159, score 65). 12 connections, 5 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D3.

## Waived Unacknowledged SAP (waived USAP), the mechanism

Waived Unacknowledged SAP (waived USAP), the mechanism is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1987-12-04; axes C3 A3 D3; 11 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: mechanism.

### What it is

The waived unacknowledged special access program is the most restrictive reporting category in the American national-security system. It is created when the Secretary of Defense, acting under 10 U.S.C. 119(e), waives the statutory requirement that specified information about a special access program appear in the reports to Congress, and instead provides that information and the justification for the waiver jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the six defense committees. That is twelve members of Congress.

### Why it is implicated

This is the mechanism that makes the board's premise legally possible without anyone committing a crime. A waived unacknowledged SAP is a program whose existence is affirmatively not acknowledged to anyone not cleared for it, whose contents are reported to twelve members rather than six bodies, whose professional staff are excluded by regulation, and whose contractor personnel are barred by DoD directive from taking material to any member of Congress without the approval of the DoD SAPCO director. Compounding this, the annual reporting requirements in 10 U.S.C. 119(a) and (b), the very reports the waiver operates on, terminated by operation of law on December 31, 2021 under section 1061 of Pub. L. 114-328, so the statutory baseline against which a waiver would be visible no longer runs. If a legacy recovery and reverse-engineering effort exists and has been kept from Congress, this is the container it fits in, and the container was designed and is administered lawfully.

### The longer account

Read 10 U.S.C. 119 in order and the architecture assembles itself. Subsection (a) requires the Secretary of Defense to submit an annual report by March 1 on special access programs, including for each SAP a brief description, major milestones, actual cost by fiscal year, and estimated total and out-year costs. Subsection (b) requires a separate report by February 1 covering each new SAP, notice of the designation and the justification for it, plus the current total program cost estimate and, notably, "an identification of existing programs or technologies that are similar to the technology, or that have a mission similar to the mission, of the program that is the subject of the notice." Subsection (c) requires 14 days' advance notice of any classification change or planned declassification. Subsection (f) forbids initiating a SAP until the defense committees are notified and thirty days elapse. Subsection (g) defines "defense committees" as the Armed Services and Appropriations Committees, and the defense appropriations subcommittees, of both chambers, six bodies.

Then subsection (e) removes it. "The Secretary of Defense may waive any requirement under subsection (a), (b), or (c) that certain information be included in a report under that subsection if the Secretary determines that inclusion of that information in the report would adversely affect the national security. Any such waiver shall be made on a case-by-case basis." And paragraph (2): if the Secretary exercises that authority, the information and the justification go "jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees." Four committees, two members each. Eight.

The Department's implementing regulations are more specific than the statute. DoD Instruction 5205.11, reissued 12 September 2024, states at paragraph 1.2.f: "The Secretary of Defense may designate select unacknowledged SAPs as 'waived' when it is determined that using standard procedures for reporting to Congress would adversely affect national security, pursuant to Section 119 of Title 10, United States Code. Waived SAPs remain subject to congressional oversight." That last sentence is the denial, and it is doing a great deal of work. The glossary of the same instruction defines a waived SAP as "A SAP for which the Secretary of Defense has waived applicable reporting following a determination of adverse effect to national security. An unacknowledged SAP that has more restrictive reporting and access controls than other unacknowledged SAPs", and an unacknowledged SAP as "A SAP having enhanced security measures, ensuring the existence of the program is not acknowledged, affirmed, or made known to any person not authorized for such information."

Section 7 of DoDI 5205.11 governs congressional access and is where the twelve-member construct becomes operational. Paragraph 7.2.a(3): "Only the chair, the ranking member, the staff directors, and one designated security manager of the defense and intelligence committees will be authorized access to waived DoD SAPs within their committee's respective oversight jurisdiction. Requests to access additional members of Congress or professional staff must be approved by the Secretary of Defense or Deputy Secretary of Defense." Paragraph 7.2.a(2) closes a gap that would otherwise leak: when a member is granted access to a SAP umbrella, that access carries down to all compartments and sub-compartments "with the exception of waived sub-compartments." Paragraph 7.2.b confirms that committee members get access to all DoD SAPs in their jurisdiction "except for waived programs." Paragraph 7.2.c(1) allows one majority and one minority professional staff member per committee access only to non-waived SAPs. Paragraph 7.2.f: "The personal staff of a member of Congress will not be granted access to DoD SAPs."

So the practical picture: a waived unacknowledged SAP is briefed to twelve members plus staff directors and a security manager. Those members cannot discuss it with their own staff, cannot discuss it with colleagues, and cannot describe it publicly. The oversight that exists is the personal, unstaffed attention of eight busy legislators who cannot take notes out of the room.

Two further features deserve an analyst's attention. First, the sunset. The note to 10 U.S.C. 119 records that for termination, effective December 31, 2021, of the provisions in subsections (a) and (b) requiring submittal of an annual report to Congress, see section 1061 of Pub. L. 114-328. The reports the waiver was designed to redact stopped being statutorily required at the end of 2021. Whatever reporting occurs now occurs as a matter of practice, not obligation.

Second, the covert-action escape hatch. The Gang of Eight construct familiar from intelligence law lives at 50 U.S.C. 3093(c)(2): when the President determines it essential to limit access to a covert action finding, the finding may be reported to the chairmen and ranking members of the intelligence committees plus the four congressional leaders. But 50 U.S.C. 3093(e) defines "covert action" as an activity to influence political, economic or military conditions abroad, and expressly excludes "traditional counterintelligence activities, traditional activities to improve or maintain the operational security of United States Government programs, or administrative activities," as well as "traditional diplomatic or military activities or routine support to such activities." A domestic recovery, custody and exploitation effort, material retrieval on U.S. soil, laboratory analysis, contractor engineering, is not an activity to influence conditions abroad. It is therefore not a covert action, it generates no Presidential finding, it triggers no reporting under 3093 at all, and the twelve-member intelligence-committee mechanism never engages. The only mechanism that engages is 10 U.S.C. 119, and 119(e) waives it.

That is the argument in its full form. Nothing in it requires anyone to have broken a law.

### What it has denied

The Department's only public position on this mechanism is the single sentence in DoDI 5205.11 paragraph 1.2.f: "Waived SAPs remain subject to congressional oversight." That assertion is true in the sense that twelve members are notified and false in the sense that ordinary committee process, staff review, and institutional memory are excluded by regulation in the same document.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, funding, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- How many waived DoD SAPs exist. The number has never been made public in any form, aggregate or otherwise.
- Whether the Secretary of Defense has ever exercised 10 U.S.C. 119(e) with respect to a UAP-related program, and if so on what date and under which Secretary.
- What replaced the 10 U.S.C. 119(a) and (b) annual reports after their statutory termination on December 31, 2021, and whether any comprehensive SAP inventory is now provided to Congress as a matter of obligation rather than practice.
- Whether a program predating the 1987 enactment of section 119 was ever brought into the SAP reporting system at all, or whether legacy arrangements were grandfathered.
- How a program funded through contractor independent research and development indirect expense, rather than a DoD program element, would appear in any 10 U.S.C. 119 report, if at all.
- Whether the twelve recipients under 119(e)(2) are given the same material as the six bodies would have received, or a summary.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 22 of 1159, score 104). 13 connections, 5 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** 'Waived' is a statutory term of art traceable to a single sentence: 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(2) substitutes, for the ordinary committee report, delivery of the information and the waiver justification 'jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees.' Nothing in section 119 requires that this delivery be written, recorded, retained, or shared with other members. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USCODE-2023-title10/html/USCODE-2023-title10-subtitleA-partI-chap2-sec119.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(b)(2)(B) requires each new-SAP report to include "an identification of existing programs or technologies that are similar to the technology, or that have a mission similar to the mission, of the program that is the subject of the notice", a comparative-technology disclosure that a 119(e) waiver can remove. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(1) provides: "The Secretary of Defense may waive any requirement under subsection (a), (b), or (c) that certain information be included in a report under that subsection if the Secretary determines that inclusion of that information in the report would adversely affect the national security. Any such waiver shall be made on a case-by-case basis." Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(2) provides that when the waiver authority is exercised, the Secretary shall provide the withheld information and the justification for the waiver "jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees", and 119(g) defines the defense committees as the Armed Services and Appropriations Committees plus the defense appropriations subcommittees of both chambers, i.e. six bodies and twelve members. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. 119(f) prohibits initiating a special access program until the defense committees are notified and 30 days elapse after notification. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim
- **Tier 1 documented.** 50 U.S.C. 3093(c)(2) is the statutory Gang of Eight provision: where the President determines it essential to limit access to a covert action finding, the finding may be reported to the chairmen and ranking minority members of the intelligence committees, the Speaker and minority leader of the House, and the majority and minority leaders of the Senate. Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/50/3093
- **Tier 1 documented.** 50 U.S.C. 3093(c)(5)(B) requires that not later than 180 days after a limited-access statement of reasons is submitted, the President either give all members of the intelligence committees access to the finding or submit a renewed statement of reasons for continued limitation, a sunset discipline that has no counterpart in 10 U.S.C. 119(e). Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/50/3093
- **Tier 1 documented.** 50 U.S.C. 3093(e) defines covert action as activity "to influence political, economic, or military conditions abroad" and expressly excludes "traditional counterintelligence activities, traditional activities to improve or maintain the operational security of United States Government programs, or administrative activities" and "traditional diplomatic or military activities or routine support to such activities", so a domestic material recovery, custody and exploitation effort would generate no Presidential finding and no reporting obligation under section 3093 at all. Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/50/3093
- **Tier 1 documented.** Administrative plumbing specific to waived programs exists in the same instruction, which again indicates live use rather than dormant authority. Footnote 5 reads verbatim: 'CA and OA SAPCO Directors may delegate AAA for non-waived SAPs to the DoD Component's SAPCO staff. AAA for waived SAPs may be delegated to the SAPCO Deputy Director or alternate designee.' Footnote 6 reads verbatim: 'DoD SAPCO will coordinate on PARs for judicial and other federal agency personnel for waived programs.' A department does not write delegation rules and a judicial-access coordination rule for an empty set. Source: DoD Instruction 5205.11, 'Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD Special Access Programs', 12 September 2024, cleared for public release - https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoD Instruction 5205.11, September 12, 2024, paragraph 1.2.f states: "The Secretary of Defense may designate select unacknowledged SAPs as 'waived' when it is determined that using standard procedures for reporting to Congress would adversely affect national security, pursuant to Section 119 of Title 10, United States Code. Waived SAPs remain subject to congressional oversight." Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 (2010) para 4.f limits access to waived SAPs, absent SecDef or DepSecDef approval, to 'the chair, the ranking minority member, and the staff directors of the defense and intelligence committees'. Source: DoDD 5205.07 (2010), para 4.f
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 Enclosure 2 requires DoD SAPCO to establish 'the criteria for annual reviews of waived DoD SAPs by the SSWG and SRG.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, Enclosure 2, para 2.m
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 Enclosure 3 provides that the SRG 'Ensures the accuracy of the SAP list submitted to Congress prior to submission of the SAP annual report.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, Enclosure 3, para 3.b(5)
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 Enclosure 3 provides that the Senior Review Group 'Reviews all waived DoD SAPs annually. Provides recommendations to the SAPOC concerning continued waived status.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, Enclosure 3, para 3.b(4)
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 defines a waived SAP as "A SAP for which the Secretary of Defense has waived applicable reporting following a determination of adverse effect to national security. An unacknowledged SAP that has more restrictive reporting and access controls than other unacknowledged SAPs." Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf (Glossary, G.2)
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 defines an unacknowledged SAP as "A SAP having enhanced security measures, ensuring the existence of the program is not acknowledged, affirmed, or made known to any person not authorized for such information." Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf (Glossary, G.2)
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 paragraph 7.2.a(2) provides that access to a SAP umbrella conveys access to all subordinate compartments and sub-compartments "with the exception of waived sub-compartments", meaning a waived sub-compartment can sit inside a program a member has otherwise been read into. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 paragraph 7.2.a(3) states: "Only the chair, the ranking member, the staff directors, and one designated security manager of the defense and intelligence committees will be authorized access to waived DoD SAPs within their committee's respective oversight jurisdiction. Requests to access additional members of Congress or professional staff must be approved by the Secretary of Defense or Deputy Secretary of Defense." Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 paragraph 7.2.c(1) limits committee professional staff access to non-waived SAPs only, and paragraph 7.2.f states flatly: "The personal staff of a member of Congress will not be granted access to DoD SAPs." Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** GAO HAS DESCRIBED THE WAIVER MECHANISM BUT HAS NEVER PUBLICLY REPORTED AN INSTANCE OF ITS USE. GAO/NSIAD-93-78, the unclassified summary of a classified report, describes the mechanism verbatim: 'Section 119, 10 U.S. Code, enacted in 1987, requires DOD to submit annual reports to the congressional defense committees on special access programs. Under the law, the Secretary of Defense may waive the requirement that certain information on special access programs be included in the annual report if he determines that the inclusion would adversely affect national security. However, he must provide the excluded information to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the congressional defense committees.' The report then never returns to the subject. Its findings on waivers are about a different thing entirely - 'DOD used waivers and exemptions to limit review, documentation, and reporting requirements' refers to Defense Acquisition Board reviews, competition requirements and Selected Acquisition Reports, not to Section 119(e). Do not let these two senses of 'waiver' be conflated on the board. Source: GAO/NSIAD-93-78, 'Defense Acquisition: Oversight of Special Access Programs Has Increased', 21 December 1992 - https://www.gao.gov/assets/nsiad-93-78.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** HISTORICAL CORROBORATION FROM A CONGRESSIONALLY CHARTERED COMMISSION. The Report of the Commission on Protecting and Reducing Government Secrecy (1997), Chapter II, states verbatim: 'Among black programs, further distinction is made for "waived" programs, considered to be so sensitive that they are exempt from standard reporting requirements to the Congress. The chairperson, ranking member, and, on occasion, other members and staff of relevant Congressional committees are notified only orally of the existence of these programs.' The same passage gives the surrounding population: 'There are approximately 150 DoD-approved SAPs (the exact number is classified and others have been created but not yet formally approved), down from 200 in the late 1980s, and roughly 300 SCI compartments, compared with an estimated 800 in the late 1980s.' The Commission gives no number for the waived subset. The phrase 'notified only orally' is the operationally important one: it describes a practice under which the 119(e)(2) delivery leaves no paper. Source: Report of the Commission on Protecting and Reducing Government Secrecy (the Moynihan Commission), S. Doc. 105-2, 1997, Chapter II, as mirrored by the Federation of American Scientists - https://sgp.fas.org/library/moynihan/chap2.html
- **Tier 1 documented.** THE PUBLIC TAXONOMY HAS BEEN NARROWING. DoD Directive 5205.07, 'Special Access Program Policy', was reissued effective 12 September 2024 and cancels the 1 July 2010 version. The full public text of the 2024 directive - purpose, applicability, policy, all thirteen responsibility assignments, the governance section, the complete glossary and the reference list - contains no occurrence of the words 'waived' or 'unacknowledged' at all. The directive that used to define the waived category no longer mentions it; the taxonomy now survives publicly only in the subordinate instruction, DoDI 5205.11. Anyone checking only the directive would conclude the category no longer exists. Source: DoD Directive 5205.07, 'Special Access Program Policy', effective 12 September 2024, cleared for public release - https://www.esd.whs.mil/portals/54/documents/dd/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** THE STRONGEST SINGLE PIECE OF EVIDENCE THAT THE POPULATION IS NON-EMPTY IS A RECURRING REVIEW REQUIREMENT. DoDI 5205.11 requires that in its annual review the Special Access Program Oversight Committee - chaired by the Deputy Secretary of Defense - shall assess, among four listed items, '(d) All waived DoD SAPs to determine the need for continued waived status.' A standing annual agenda item to decide whether waived programs should REMAIN waived is only drafted if waived programs exist. This is a 2024 document, so the finding is current, not historical. Source: DoD Instruction 5205.11, 'Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD Special Access Programs', 12 September 2024, cleared for public release - https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Department's own definition, from the DoDI 5205.11 glossary, verbatim: 'waived SAP - A SAP for which the Secretary of Defense has waived applicable reporting following a determination of adverse effect to national security. An unacknowledged SAP that has more restrictive reporting and access controls than other unacknowledged SAPs.' And, adjacent to it: 'unacknowledged SAP - A SAP having enhanced security measures, ensuring the existence of the program is not acknowledged, affirmed, or made known to any person not authorized for such information.' A waived SAP is therefore a proper subset of the unacknowledged SAPs, distinguished precisely by the exercise of the Section 119(e)(1) waiver. Source: DoD Instruction 5205.11, 'Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD Special Access Programs', 12 September 2024, cleared for public release - https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The annual reporting requirements of 10 U.S.C. 119(a) and (b) terminated effective December 31, 2021, pursuant to section 1061 of Pub. L. 114-328, as recorded in the Termination of Reporting Requirements note to section 119. Source: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title10-section119&num=0&edition=prelim (note: Termination of Reporting Requirements)
