## Carve-Out Provision (DCSA relief from NISP oversight)

Carve-Out Provision (DCSA relief from NISP oversight) is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1993-01-06; axes C3 A3 D3; 10 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: mechanism.

### What it is

A "carve-out" is the regulatory provision under which the Director of the Defense Counterintelligence and Security Agency is relieved of industrial security oversight for a Department of Defense special access program. It is codified at 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3), the National Industrial Security Program Operating Manual rule, and exercised in accordance with DoD Directive 5205.07, DoD Special Access Program Policy. Approval authority rests with exactly two officials: the Secretary of Defense or the Deputy Secretary of Defense.

### Why it is implicated

The carve-out is the mechanism that makes a concealed program administratively possible, and it is written down in the Code of Federal Regulations. Every cleared contractor facility in the United States is subject to DCSA oversight under the NISP; the carve-out is the single lawful exception, and once approved, DCSA retains cognizance only over the baseline facility clearance and not over the program. That means the government's own industrial security inspectorate, the body that would otherwise know which contractor holds what, in which vault, under which contract, is by design blind to the program. DoDD 5205.07 layers a second control on top: "No DoD contractor entity, contractor employee, contractor representative, or consultant will provide SAP material to any members of Congress or their staff without Director, DoD SAPCO approval." A contractor holding carve-out material therefore faces an affirmative regulatory prohibition on telling Congress, enforced by an office whose director reports directly to the Deputy Secretary of Defense. And the statutory backstop weakened rather than strengthened: the annual special access program reports to the defense committees required by 10 U.S.C. § 119(a) and (b) terminated by operation of law effective 31 December 2021. This is the load-bearing structural claim of the entire board, not that concealment is possible in principle, but that the specific regulatory instruments exist, are named, are current, and route around both DCSA and Congress by design.

### The longer account

Start with the ordinary case. Under Executive Order 12829 and 32 CFR Part 117, the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security acts as the Cognizant Security Agency for the Department of Defense, and DCSA serves as the DoD Cognizant Security Office. Section 117.6(b)(3) assigns DCSA the duty to "provide security oversight of the NISP as the DoD CSO on behalf of DoD components and those non-DoD executive branch agencies who enter into agreements with DoD." Then comes the exception, in the same sentence-pair: "The Director, DCSA, will be relieved of this oversight function for DoD special access programs (SAPs) when the Secretary of Defense or the Deputy Secretary of Defense approves a carve-out provision in accordance with DoDD 5205.07, 'DoD SAP Policy.'"

The operative definition is not in the CFR; it is in the instructions to DD Form 254, the Contract Security Classification Specification that accompanies every classified contract. Item 10f: "A 'carve-out' is a classified contract awarded by the GCA in connection with a SAP for which DCSA has been relieved of security and/or oversight responsibility by the Secretary or Deputy Secretary of Defense." The instructions add two operationally decisive notes. First: "Check block 15 'Yes' if the contract is performed at the contractor facility and DCSA will be relieved of security cognizance (a carve-out), and identify the CSO for the SAP as it is responsible for inspection of the SAP. NOTE: DCSA does retain oversight responsibility for the baseline FCL at which there is an approved carve-out." Second: "Not all SAPs are 'carve-outs' because, in some instances, the program or supporting SAP Security Office will allow the DCSA CSO to retain inspection responsibility." Read together: at a carved-out facility, DCSA still validates the company's baseline clearance but has no visibility into the program, and the program's own security office designates the inspecting authority. The inspector is chosen by the inspected.

DoDD 5205.07 was reissued on 12 September 2024, approved by Deputy Secretary of Defense Kathleen H. Hicks. It cancels the July 1, 2010 directive and incorporates and cancels the Deputy Secretary of Defense Memorandum "Implementation Guidance for DoD Special Access Program Enterprise Reform," 11 July 2023. Its governance architecture is a closed loop. The DoD SAP Central Office director sits under the authority, direction and control of the Deputy Secretary of Defense and reports directly to him or her; is Executive Secretary of the Special Access Program Oversight Committee, which the Deputy Secretary chairs; is Executive Secretary of the Senior Review Group; chairs the SAP Senior Working Group; is delegated TOP SECRET original classification authority for all DoD SAP information for purposes of issuing security classification guides; accredits congressional SAP facilities; and maintains the records of what SAP information Congress has been given. Establishment of new SAP umbrellas and compartments "will be by exception," and establishment of SAPs to protect new and novel critical program information "at all tiers of the SAP architecture requires Secretary of Defense or Deputy Secretary of Defense approval."

DCSA's residual role under the same directive is limited to two lines in section 2.7: develop SAP-related training courses and maintain a cadre of proficient personnel, and support professional development and certification of SAP security professionals. The agency that oversees industrial security for the rest of the defense industrial base is, inside the SAP enterprise, a training provider.

The congressional-notification provision is the one that converts an administrative arrangement into a concealment mechanism. Section 3.2.g of DoDD 5205.07 requires all personnel accessed to DoD SAPs to notify the SAPCO director when briefing or providing SAP material to a member of Congress or staff, and then states flatly: "No DoD contractor entity, contractor employee, contractor representative, or consultant will provide SAP material to any members of Congress or their staff without Director, DoD SAPCO approval." A contractor employee at a carved-out facility who believes a program has been improperly withheld from Congress is, by this directive, prohibited from doing anything about it without permission from the office nearest the Deputy Secretary.

Statute does less work here than most people assume. 10 U.S.C. § 119 required annual reports on special access programs to the defense committees under subsections (a) and (b), but a note to the section records that those reporting requirements terminated effective 31 December 2021 pursuant to section 1061 of Public Law 114-328. Subsection (e) permits the Secretary of Defense to waive inclusion of specified information in a report on national security grounds, in which case the information and the justification go "jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees", a set of twelve members across six bodies. Subsection (f) forbids initiating a SAP until the defense committees are notified and thirty days elapse; there is no enforcement provision, no penalty, and no auditing body outside the executive branch positioned to detect a program that was never notified. 10 U.S.C. § 119a governs the parallel category of alternative compensatory control measures.

One loose thread in the directive itself is worth flagging to a downstream analyst. Section 2.1.a(6) makes the SAPCO director "Executive Secretary of the Special Technical Activities Oversight Committee pursuant to the November 21, 2020 Deputy Secretary of Defense Memorandum," while the directive's own reference list names that memorandum "Sensitive Technical Activities Oversight Committee (STAOC) Charter," 21 November 2020. The body is named twice, differently, in a single 2024 directive. That committee's charter has not been released.

### What it has denied

There is no denial here, and that is the point: the carve-out is publicly promulgated regulation, published on the Directives Division website and cleared for public release. The Department has never had to deny the existence of a mechanism that relieves its own security inspectorate of oversight, because the mechanism is printed in the Code of Federal Regulations and in the instructions to a form every classified contract carries.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, oversight, funding, custody.

### Open questions on this node

- How many DoD SAPs currently hold approved carve-out provisions? The number has never been published, and 32 CFR 117.6 imposes no counting or reporting requirement.
- Who are the Cognizant Security Offices designated to inspect carved-out programs in lieu of DCSA, and are any of them staffed by the contractor's own personnel?
- The DepSecDef memorandum chartering the Special/Sensitive Technical Activities Oversight Committee (21 November 2020) has not been publicly released. What does it cover?
- What replaced the 10 U.S.C. § 119(a)-(b) annual SAP reports after they terminated on 31 December 2021, if anything?
- Does any statute or directive require notification to Congress of the approval of a carve-out provision itself, as distinct from establishment of the underlying SAP?
- DoDD 5205.07 §3.2.g prohibits contractors from providing SAP material to Congress without SAPCO approval. How is that provision reconciled with whistleblower protections and with AARO's claimed statutory access?
- Is there any record of a carve-out provision ever being denied or revoked by a Secretary or Deputy Secretary of Defense?
- Which office audits whether a program that should have been notified under § 119(f) actually was? The Assistant to the Secretary of Defense for Privacy, Civil Liberties, and Transparency is assigned audit functions under DoDD 5148.13, but the scope is not specified in 5205.07.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 39 of 1159, score 88). 12 connections, 5 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. § 119(e) permits the Secretary of Defense to waive inclusion of information in SAP reports on national security grounds on a case-by-case basis, in which case the information and justification are provided "jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees", that is, to twelve members across the Senate and House Armed Services and Appropriations Committees. Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/10/119 (10 U.S.C. § 119(e), (g))
- **Tier 1 documented.** 10 U.S.C. § 119(f) provides that a special access program may not be initiated until the defense committees are notified and thirty days elapse; the statute prescribes no penalty for non-notification and assigns no external auditor. Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/10/119 (10 U.S.C. § 119(f))
- **Tier 1 documented.** 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3) provides: "The Director, DCSA, will be relieved of this oversight function for DoD special access programs (SAPs) when the Secretary of Defense or the Deputy Secretary of Defense approves a carve-out provision in accordance with DoDD 5205.07, 'DoD SAP Policy.'" Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/32/117.6 (32 CFR § 117.6, Responsibilities, paragraph (b)(3))
- **Tier 1 documented.** 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3): 'The Director, DCSA, will be relieved of this oversight function for DoD special access programs (SAPs) when the Secretary of Defense or the Deputy Secretary of Defense approves a carve-out provision in accordance with DoDD 5205.07, "DoD SAP Policy".' Source: 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3)
- **Tier 1 documented.** At a carved-out facility DCSA's residual visibility is limited to the baseline facility clearance: "NOTE: DCSA does retain oversight responsibility for the baseline FCL at which there is an approved carve-out." Inspection of the SAP itself is performed by a CSO identified by the SAP. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/portals/54/documents/dd/forms/dd/dd0254-Inst.pdf (DD Form 254 Instructions, Item 10f(4))
- **Tier 1 documented.** Carve-out status is discretionary and program-controlled: "Not all SAPs are 'carve-outs' because, in some instances, the program or supporting SAP Security Office will allow the DCSA CSO to retain inspection responsibility." Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/portals/54/documents/dd/forms/dd/dd0254-Inst.pdf (DD Form 254 Instructions, Item 15 guidance)
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 names the same oversight body inconsistently within a single document: §2.1.a(6) calls it the "Special Technical Activities Oversight Committee" while the References section cites the Deputy Secretary of Defense Memorandum, "Sensitive Technical Activities Oversight Committee (STAOC) Charter," 21 November 2020. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf (DoDD 5205.07, §2.1.a(6) and References)
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 section 3.2.g provides: "No DoD contractor entity, contractor employee, contractor representative, or consultant will provide SAP material to any members of Congress or their staff without Director, DoD SAPCO approval." Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf (DoDD 5205.07, 12 September 2024, §3.2.g)
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 was reissued effective 12 September 2024, approved by Deputy Secretary of Defense Kathleen H. Hicks; it reissues and cancels DoDD 5205.07 of 1 July 2010 as amended, and incorporates and cancels the Deputy Secretary of Defense Memorandum "Implementation Guidance for DoD Special Access Program Enterprise Reform," 11 July 2023. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf (DoDD 5205.07, 12 September 2024, cover page)
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 §3.1.d provides that "Establishment of new SAP umbrellas and compartments will be by exception. Establishment of SAPs to protect new and novel CPI at all tiers of the SAP architecture requires Secretary of Defense or Deputy Secretary of Defense approval." Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf (DoDD 5205.07, §3.1.d)
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 (12 Sep 2024) glossary defines it: 'carve out: A provision approved by the Secretary of Defense or Deputy Secretary of Defense that relieves DCSA of its National Industrial Security Program obligation.' Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 Enclosure 6 lists 'Proposals to relieve DSS of NISP security oversight responsibilities (carve-out) when required' among the actions that must be coordinated through the DoD SAPCO to the Deputy Secretary of Defense for decision. Source: DoDI 5205.11, Enclosure 6, para 5.a(8)
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 requires DoD SAPCO to provide 'DSS confirmation of SAP facilities where the DSS has been carved out of security oversight functions', which means a list of carved-out facilities exists as an agency record. Source: DoDI 5205.11, Enclosure 2, para 2.k
- **Tier 1 documented.** MAJOR CHANGE, 2024. DoDI 5205.11 para 1.2.i.: 'Defense Counterintelligence and Security Agency (DCSA) oversight for the administration of the National Industrial Security Program will be phased out in July 2029 in accordance with the carve-out provision.' The 2024 policy therefore schedules a blanket, enterprise-wide withdrawal of DCSA from DoD SAP industrial security by July 2029, rather than a case-by-case carve-out. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf

Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf (DoDD 5205.07, §2.1.b and §2.1.e)
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Director, DoD SAPCO, is under the authority, direction and control of the Deputy Secretary of Defense, reports directly to the Deputy Secretary, serves as Executive Secretary of the SAP Oversight Committee (which the Deputy Secretary chairs) and of the Senior Review Group, and chairs the SAP Senior Working Group. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf (DoDD 5205.07, §1.2.e, §2.1.a(2), §3.2.a(2))
- **Tier 1 documented.** The annual special access program reporting requirements of 10 U.S.C. § 119(a) and (b) terminated effective 31 December 2021 pursuant to section 1061 of Public Law 114-328, per the Termination of Reporting Requirements note to the section. Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/uscode/text/10/119 (Statutory Notes, Termination of Reporting Requirements)
- **Tier 1 documented.** The carve-out is approvable only by the Secretary of Defense or the Deputy Secretary of Defense - no delegation to a service secretary, component head or SAPCO director is provided for in DoDD 5205.07 (2010), DoDI 5205.11 or DoDM 5205.07 Volume 1. Source: DoDD 5205.07 (2010) Glossary; DoDM 5205.07-V1 Enclosure 2 para 2.c; 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3)
- **Tier 1 documented.** The carve-out's authority runs to exactly two officials. 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3) conditions it on approval by 'the Secretary of Defense or the Deputy Secretary of Defense', and on no one else. Source: 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3), eCFR renderer API, read 22 August 2026, https://www.ecfr.gov/api/renderer/v1/content/enhanced/current/title-32?part=117
- **Tier 1 documented.** The operative definition is in the DD Form 254 Instructions: "A 'carve-out' is a classified contract awarded by the GCA in connection with a SAP for which DCSA has been relieved of security and/or oversight responsibility by the Secretary or Deputy Secretary of Defense (DoDD 5205.07, 'Special Access Program (SAP) Policy')." Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/portals/54/documents/dd/forms/dd/dd0254-Inst.pdf (DD Form 254 Instructions, April 2018, updated 20250711, Item 10f)
- **Tier 1 documented.** The regulation ties the carve-out to DoDD 5205.07, 'DoD SAP Policy', by name and by URL, so the codified rule and the DoD directive are a matched pair rather than parallel authorities. Source: 32 CFR 117.6(b)(3), eCFR renderer API, read 22 August 2026, https://www.ecfr.gov/api/renderer/v1/content/enhanced/current/title-32?part=117
- **Tier 1 documented.** The regulatory counterpart, 32 CFR 117.6(b): 'The Director, DCSA, will be relieved of this oversight function for DoD special access programs (SAPs) when the Secretary of Defense or the Deputy Secretary of Defense approves a carve-out provision in accordance with DoDD 5205.07.' Source: https://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-32/subtitle-A/chapter-I/subchapter-D/part-117/section-117.6
- **Tier 1 documented.** Under DoDD 5205.07 §2.7 the Director, DCSA has only two SAP responsibilities: developing SAP-related training courses and maintaining a proficient cadre, and supporting professional development and certification of SAP security professionals. DCSA has no inspection or oversight function assigned in the directive. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf (DoDD 5205.07, §2.7)

## STAR GATE and predecessor remote viewing programs, 1970-1995

STAR GATE and predecessor remote viewing programs, 1970-1995 is a event on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1970 to 1995; axes C0 A3 D2; 10 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: CIA/Army/DIA psychoenergetics effort; SCANATE, GONDOLA WISH, GRILL FLAME, CENTER LANE, SUN STREAK, STAR GATE.

### What it is

STAR GATE was the final code name for a U.S. government psychic-intelligence effort that ran from roughly 1970 to 1995 under a rotating series of names and host agencies: SCANATE (CIA-funded, from 1970), GONDOLA WISH (Army ACSI, 1977), GRILL FLAME (Army INSCOM Detachment G at Fort Meade, 1978), INSCOM CENTER LANE (1983), SUN STREAK (DIA Scientific and Technical Intelligence Directorate, office code DT-S, 1985), and STAR GATE (DIA with SAIC as prime contractor, 1991). It combined an operational collection unit that tasked trained remote viewers against real intelligence targets with a separately maintained contract research program run out of Stanford Research Institute. It was terminated in 1995 after a Congressionally Directed Action moved it to CIA and CIA declined to keep it.

### Why it is implicated

STAR GATE is the board's cleanest declassified proof-of-concept for the exact institutional pattern the legacy program is alleged to follow: a real, funded, operational, compartmented effort into a phenomenon the government publicly treated as nonsense, sustained across two decades and at least four host organizations by serial renaming, moved between Army, DIA and CIA in a way that defeats continuity of oversight, and run through a cleared contractor (SRI, then SAIC) rather than in-house. The 1995 CIA program-status memorandum shows the disposal mechanics with unusual clarity: CIA and DIA had already made 'plans to declassify all but the most sensitive aspects of the program' before any evaluation was complete, and the memo's author recommended asking Congress to agree the program should NOT transfer to CIA before the contracted review had even begun. That is a decision to terminate reached in advance of the evidence, followed by a curated declassification, which means the archive the public got is the residue of a deliberate sorting operation rather than a data dump. The program also demonstrates that a phenomenon-adjacent effort could be carried in the black for twenty-five years while officials denied its existence, and that its closure could be framed publicly as a scientific verdict rather than a bureaucratic one. Finally, STAR GATE is a personnel bridge: the scientists, contractors and case officers it credentialed are the same names that reappear in the NIDS, AAWSAP and To The Stars-era record, giving the board a documented lineage from Cold War compartmented research to the 2017-2026 disclosure fight.

### The longer account

The origin story is threat-mirroring. Between 1969 and 1971, U.S. intelligence concluded the Soviet Union was investing seriously in what it called psychotronics, with estimates running to 60 million rubles per year by 1970 and over 300 million by 1975. Whether or not the Soviets had anything, the money itself was treated as evidence, and CIA opened a research line in 1970 under the name SCANATE. In 1972 the research moved to Stanford Research Institute in Menlo Park, where Russell Targ and Harold Puthoff ran it, initially around a small set of gifted individuals including the artist Ingo Swann. The client requirement, per the FAS program file, was a minimum accuracy of 65 percent.

The operational side grew separately out of the Army. GONDOLA WISH, a 1977 Army Assistant Chief of Staff for Intelligence effort under the Systems Exploitation Detachment, was chartered to evaluate potential adversary applications of remote viewing. It became a collection project in mid-1978 as GRILL FLAME, INSCOM 'Detachment G,' housed in buildings 2560 and 2561 at Fort Meade, Maryland. The SRI research was folded into GRILL FLAME in early 1979, and hundreds of experiments were run at SRI through 1986. Renamed the INSCOM CENTER LANE Project in 1983, the unit adopted a written methodology developed by Swann and Puthoff meant to make the capability trainable rather than innate. Its existence surfaced publicly when columnist Jack Anderson reported on it in April 1984. That same year the National Academy of Sciences' National Research Council evaluated remote viewing for the Army Research Institute and returned an unfavorable verdict. When Army funding ended in late 1985, the unit did not die; it was redesignated SUN STREAK and moved to DIA's Scientific and Technical Intelligence Directorate under office code DT-S. In 1991 the contract moved to Science Applications International Corporation and the program became STAR GATE, downgraded from a Special Access Program to a LIMDIS, limited dissemination, program. Edwin May presided over roughly 70 percent of the total contractor budget and 85 percent of data collection. Over two decades the effort consumed on the order of $20 million, with about $11 million spent from the mid-1980s to the early 1990s, and involved more than forty personnel including about twenty-three remote viewers.

The end is where the concealment mechanics are legible. The FY1995 Defense Appropriations bill directed transfer to CIA and directed CIA to conduct a retrospective review. What happened next is documented in a SECRET memorandum, 'Star Gate - Program Status and Proposed New Direction,' now in the CIA reading room as CIA-RDP96-00791R000100160001-0. The Executive Director approved CIA's approach in late January 1995; it was transmitted to Congress and briefed to Richard D'Amato of the Senate Appropriations Committee staff on 27 March 1995. CIA transferred funds to the Army Research Institute to make it executive agent for a National Research Council blue-ribbon review. In parallel, and this is the sentence that matters for the board, CIA was 'working closely with DIA and their support contractor to identify all relevant documents pertaining to Star Gate' and had 'made plans to declassify all but the most sensitive aspects of the program.' The sorting of what the public would eventually see was underway before the verdict was in.

NRC declined the review by letter dated 5 May 1995, citing its own 1988 report and quoting its conclusion that the case for remote viewing was 'not just very weak, but virtually nonexistent.' NRC referred CIA to Dr. David Goslin, president of the American Institutes for Research. The memo's author then recommended skipping the review entirely: 'in view of the strong position taken by the NRC... I want to propose at this point that we strongly consider the option of going back to Congress with the recommendation not to accept the Star Gate program from DIA,' adding that even if AIR proceeded 'there exists a very strong possibility that AIR will merely reach the same conclusion firmly held by the NRC.' He also recorded that 'neither the DO nor the DI were at all interested in having this program reside in their directorates,' and deferred any decision about the operational personnel still at DIA. The recommendation was explicit: 'ask Congress to agree that the program should NOT be transferred to CIA.'

AIR did the review anyway. Its final report is dated 29 September 1995 and was released publicly on 28 November 1995. It contained two irreconcilable assessments, statistician Jessica Utts finding a statistically significant laboratory effect and psychologist Ray Hyman finding none, and a single recommendation to terminate. CIA's public line was that there was no case in which ESP had provided data used to guide intelligence operations. The program had been sustained politically for years by Senator Claiborne Pell of Rhode Island and Representative Charles Rose of North Carolina; with the program dead, the constituency dissolved.

What the board should take from STAR GATE is not whether remote viewing worked. It is the demonstrated administrative repertoire: serial renaming across host commands, movement between Army, DIA and CIA, SAP-to-LIMDIS reclassification, contractor intermediation, a pre-cooked termination decision, a curated declassification, and a public verdict framed in the language of science rather than of budget politics. Every one of those moves is available to any program that wants to disappear while remaining funded, and every one is documented here in the government's own paper.

### What it has denied

CIA's public position at termination was that remote viewing had never produced data used to guide an intelligence operation and that the program was scientifically unsupported, a verdict delivered through a contracted outside evaluator rather than in CIA's own voice. The 1995 internal memorandum shows that position was arrived at before the evaluation was contracted, and that the declassification package was scoped in parallel by CIA and DIA to exclude 'the most sensitive aspects of the program.'

Roles the board assigns it: research, concealment, funding, oversight, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- What material was withheld under the 1995 decision to declassify 'all but the most sensitive aspects of the program,' and under whose signature was the withholding line drawn?
- Which DIA support contractor performed the 1995 document identification and declassification sorting alongside CIA?
- Did any part of the operational capability survive termination under a different name or host, and where does the FY1996 funding line go?
- What did Richard D'Amato and the Senate Appropriations Committee actually receive in the 27 March 1995 briefing, and does a staff memorandum of that briefing exist?
- Were STAR GATE viewers ever tasked against UAP or recovered-material targets, and if so are those session records inside or outside the released CREST collection?
- What was the disposition of the operational personnel at DIA whose future the memo explicitly deferred pending the blue-ribbon panel?
- Who authored and who received the 1995 memorandum? The released copy carries a SG1I marking in place of names.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 78 of 1159, score 70). 11 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A3/D2.

## The Classified Annex (NDAA and Defense Appropriations)

The Classified Annex (NDAA and Defense Appropriations) is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1970; axes C1 A3 D3; 9 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: Unpublished companion document carrying the force of law over classified programme funding.

### What it is

The classified addenda -- variously called annexes, schedules, appendices and supplements -- attached to the three annual national security statutes (Intelligence Authorization Act, National Defense Authorization Act, Department of Defense Appropriations Act) and to the committee reports that accompany them. Together with the classified notification channels of 10 U.S.C. 119, they are the legal machinery by which Congress appropriates money and issues binding direction to programs whose existence is not public. No classified annex has ever been published, in any form, redacted or otherwise.

### Why it is implicated

If a legacy retrieval and reverse-engineering effort has been funded for eight decades, the money moved through this channel, because there is no other channel that both carries the force of law and never surfaces. The annex system is not a leak in oversight; it is a purpose-built container that gives classified direction "the legal status of public law" while keeping it in a safe in a secure room that few members ever enter. Layered on top of it, 10 U.S.C. 119(e) lets the Secretary of Defense strip specified content out of the Special Access Program reports entirely and deliver it to only twelve people -- the chair and ranking member of six bodies. And the statutory backbone that forced annual SAP disclosure at all, 10 U.S.C. 119(a) and (b), was terminated effective December 31, 2021 by a sunset provision Congress itself enacted. David Grusch's sworn answer to the funding question -- "misappropriation of funds and self-fund" -- describes an abuse that this architecture is uniquely well designed to conceal, because the annex records what Congress intended to buy but the public record cannot be reconciled against it.

### The longer account

The classified annex is the least glamorous object on this board and the most important. It is the mechanism that makes the rest of the question -- who, where, under what authority, paid for how -- answerable in principle and unanswerable in practice.

Dakota Rudesill, a former Senate Budget Committee staffer and Intelligence Community official, published the only serious empirical study of the practice. His finding: over thirty-six years Congress wrote at least 124 provisions in 68 Public Laws that can reasonably be read to give classified addenda the force of law, and produced at least 271 committee reports carrying classified addenda since 1977. About half of the 124 were incorporation provisions -- statutory text whose entire operative content is a pointer to a document nobody outside the compartment will ever see. The standard formulation appears at Section 102 of virtually every Intelligence Authorization Act: the amounts authorized and the personnel ceilings "are those specified in the classified Schedule of Authorizations." Accompanying report language routinely states that the Schedule "is incorporated by reference in the Act and has the legal status of public law." The Intelligence Community's own top lawyer has agreed with that characterization.

Rudesill is careful, and his carefulness is itself evidence. He notes that nothing is publicly known about the length of any annex -- "An individual addendum could be two pages long, or 20, or 200" -- and that each committee invites members to read the addenda in a secure room, but "few do." He also records that the practice accelerated: Congress wrote more force-of-law annex provisions in 2011-2015 than in the first eight Congresses of the practice combined, from 1977 to 1992. This is the shape of a mechanism that grew because it worked.

The second layer is statutory rather than procedural. 10 U.S.C. 119 is the SAP oversight statute. Subsection (a) requires an annual March 1 report to the six defense committees listing every DoD special access program, with descriptions, milestones, actual costs by year, and estimated costs across a six-year horizon. Subsection (b) requires a February 1 notice and justification for every new SAP. Subsection (f) forbids initiating a SAP until the defense committees are notified and thirty days elapse. On its face this is real oversight.

Subsection (e) is the escape hatch. The Secretary of Defense "may waive any requirement under subsection (a), (b), or (c) that certain information be included in a report" upon determining that inclusion "would adversely affect the national security." When he does, the information and the justification go "jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees" -- twelve members of Congress, no staff specified. DoD Instruction 5205.11 builds the corresponding category into doctrine: a waived SAP is "An unacknowledged SAP that has more restrictive reporting and access controls than other unacknowledged SAPs."

The third layer is the one almost nobody has noticed. The U.S. Code note appended to Section 119 reads: "For termination, effective Dec. 31, 2021, of provisions in subsecs. (a) and (b) of this section requiring submittal of annual report to Congress, see section 1061 of Pub. L. 114-328." The annual SAP report and the new-SAP notice -- the two provisions that produced a written, dated, comprehensive inventory of the black budget's program structure -- were swept away by a generic reporting-burden sunset enacted in the FY2017 NDAA. The subsection (e) waiver survives; the reports it was designed to abridge do not.

Against that background, Congress in December 2022 legislated a tell. 50 U.S.C. 3373b(a)(4)(B) -- Section 1673 of the FY2023 NDAA -- requires the Secretary of Defense, within 72 hours, to report to the defense and intelligence committees and to congressional leadership any authorized UAP disclosure that relates to "a restricted access activity, a special access program, or a compartmented access program that has not been explicitly and clearly reported to the congressional defense committees or the congressional intelligence committees." Congress does not write 72-hour tripwires for hypotheticals. The statute is a signed, dated legislative acknowledgment that unreported access programs are a live possibility.

AARO has since conceded, in its own words, one instance: "In 2021, without sufficient justification, the scope of an IC Controlled Access Program was expanded to protect UAP reverse-engineering." AARO adds that the program never recovered or reverse-engineered anything and was disestablished for lack of merit. Set aside the exculpatory half. The admitted half is that a controlled access program was expanded without sufficient justification, in 2021, specifically to protect UAP reverse-engineering -- which is precisely the nesting behavior Grusch described when he said agencies were "nesting UAP activities in conventional SAP/CAP programs ... without appropriate reporting to various oversight authorities."

Finally, the annex logic reappears in the disclosure statute itself. Under Section 1843 of the FY2024 NDAA, a UAP record may be withheld from the National Archives collection when "the original classification authority makes a determination that there is clear and convincing evidence" of enumerated harms. The original classification authority is the agency holding the record. The annex principle -- the withholder judges the withholding -- survived into the law written to end it.

### What it has denied

There is no denial to catalogue here, because the mechanism is openly documented in statute and doctrine and defended on its merits: the Intelligence Community's chief lawyer has publicly agreed that classified schedules carry the legal status of public law. The concealment is not denied; it is legislated.

Roles the board assigns it: funding, concealment, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether any classified annex from 1944 to the present contains a line item, program identifier or nickname corresponding to a materials-retrieval or exploitation effort -- no annex has ever been released.
- Which specific SAPs, if any, have had 10 U.S.C. 119(e) waivers applied to them, and how many waivers have been issued in total; the statute requires no public accounting.
- Whether the December 31, 2021 termination of the 119(a) and 119(b) reporting requirements was substantively debated or was a drafting artifact of a generic reporting-burden sunset.
- Whether the DoD or IC has ever complied with the 72-hour notification requirement of 50 U.S.C. 3373b(a)(4)(B), and how many such notifications have been made.
- The identity of the IC Controlled Access Program whose scope AARO says was expanded in 2021 'without sufficient justification' to protect UAP reverse-engineering, and who authorized the expansion.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 112 of 1159, score 59). 9 connections, 6 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A3/D3.

## Northrop Grumman Corporation

Northrop Grumman Corporation is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1994; axes C2 A3 D0; 7 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: Prime contractor; B-2/B-21; former Nevada Test Site management partner.

### What it is

One of the United States' prime defense contractors, builder of the B-2 and the B-21 Raider at Air Force Plant 42, and, from 2006 to 2017, the leader of National Security Technologies LLC, the consortium that held the management and operating contract for the Nevada Test Site after Bechtel Nevada.

### Why it is implicated

It sits at the intersection of the two things this board tracks: work at the deepest classification levels, and custody of the Nevada complex. The stealth bomber programmes put it inside compartmented aircraft development at Plant 42; the NSTec contract put it in operational control of the ground the retrieval and reverse-engineering claims are attached to. Senate Appropriations investigator Richard D'Amato is reported to have named it, with Lockheed Martin and EG&G, as the kind of private entity inside which a cover-up could feasibly sit. That last is a Tier 3 report and is carried as one.

### The longer account

Northrop Grumman crossed this map's floor on the build of 18 September 2026, which is ordinary churn and not a finding: the board's practice, recorded in data/l0-shorts.json, is that a corpus build pushing a node over the L0 floor gets its dossier authored. What is worth stating is that its case for the map does not rest on the edge that moved it. That edge is a Tier 2 self-stated client list, an illustrator saying he did conceptual work for the company, with no personnel record and no contract record opened, and it proves nothing about the company at all. The case rests instead on two Tier 1 claims already on its record: the DOE listing that makes it the Nevada Test Site's management and operating contractor for eleven years, and USAspending's record of prime contract obligations in the tens of billions since FY2008. A board about who holds the programme's money and the programme's ground has to carry the company that held both. The D'Amato report is the weakest thing here and the most quoted, and it is graded Tier 3 for that reason.

Roles the board assigns it: custody, concealment, funding.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether any NSTec contract document names the scope of the management and operating role at the Nevada Test Site beyond the DOE research-organization listing.
- What the USAspending prime contract obligations break down to by programme, which the recipient search does not itself show.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 410 of 1159, score 34). 7 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A3/D0.

Source: DOE/OSTI records listing Bechtel Nevada and National Security Technologies, LLC as successive Nevada Test Site research organisations
- **Tier 1 documented.** USAspending records prime contract obligations to Northrop Grumman Systems Corporation in the tens of billions of dollars since FY2008. Source: USAspending.gov recipient search, retrieved 12 August 2026

## US Army Intelligence and Security Command (INSCOM)

US Army Intelligence and Security Command (INSCOM) is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1977-01-01; axes C2 A3 D2; 6 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: Army intelligence major command; ran the psychoenergetics effort as a special access programme, 1977-1985.

### What it is

The United States Army Intelligence and Security Command, the Army's intelligence and security major command, headquartered in the period the board covers at Arlington Hall Station, Arlington, Virginia, with subordinate elements at Fort George G. Meade. Its letterhead in this period reads 'DEPARTMENT OF THE ARMY / UNITED STATES ARMY INTELLIGENCE AND SECURITY COMMAND / ARLINGTON HALL STATION / ARLINGTON, VIRGINIA 22212'. From 1977 it ran the Army's psychoenergetics effort, first as Project GRILL FLAME and then as the INSCOM CENTER LANE Project, worked out of the Security Support Detachment of the 902d Military Intelligence Group at Fort Meade.

### Why it is implicated

INSCOM is the board's cleanest documented case of a ridiculed subject held inside the machinery of a special access programme, with the paperwork surviving to prove it. CENTER LANE was not an INSCOM designation. It was 'a Secretary of the Army designated Special Access Program', and every page of the project's file carries the caveat 'CENTER LANE SPECIAL ACCESS PROGRAM RESTRICT DISSEMINATION TO THOSE WITH VERIFIED ACCESS TO CATEGORY THREE (3)'. The command's own commanding general recommended ending the work, then on 2 March 1985 notified the Department of the Army of the deactivation, of the attachment of the project's assets and resources to the Defense Intelligence Agency under operational control, and of the retirement of the nickname. Start, funding, access control, termination and handoff are all in opened documents.

### The longer account

The value of INSCOM to this board is that it settles by document what every other compartmented account has to argue by inference: that the United States has taken a subject the public conversation treats as absurd and run it at service-secretary access levels for the better part of a decade. The GDIP memorandum of 4 April 1984 says so in the command's own voice, dating the work and complaining about how it was funded in the same sentence: 'Since the beginning of USAINSCOM's exploitation of psychoenergetics (PE) technology with Project GRILL FLAME in 1977 through the present effort under CENTER LANE, the project has been conducted on an ad hoc basis, using resources taken "out of hide."' A programme paid for out of hide and controlled at category three is a precise description of the structure this board keeps asserting exists.

The origin is equally documented and equally ordinary. A 1978 talking paper records that 'ACSI & MG Rolya agreed that INSCOM would "check out" parapsychology phenomen[a], and that Army Intelligence would obligate $75 K to support an SRI Subject selection and training program for INSCOM personnel over the next year', with the contract let through AMSAA and the money buying a project manager and three subjects. The Army staff started it, the Army staff paid for it, and a nonprofit research institute in California supplied the method. That is the same shape the board traces at SRI International and later at BAASS: a government sponsor with deniability, a private research house with a security apparatus, and a small line of money.

What makes the ending as useful as the beginning is that INSCOM did not conceal the project so much as move it. The July 1984 memorandum has the commanding general writing that 'INSCOM ... should discontinue our involvement with psychoenergetic research and operations', intending 'to cease INSCOM CENTER LANE Project operations on 30 Sept 1984', and asking for authority to permit 'timely and undisrupted transfer of (1) ICLP operations to a national agency such as DIA or NSA'. The transfer happened. A capability judged not worth the Army's trouble was handed intact to a defence agency and its name retired, which is exactly how a programme becomes hard to follow without anyone destroying anything.

### What it has denied

The board records no denial link on this node in either direction, and that is the point worth stating. INSCOM never had to deny CENTER LANE, because the access controls did the work a denial would otherwise have to do: a Secretary of the Army designated special access programme with a category three caveat on every page needs no public position at all. When the command was finished with it, it did not disown the project; it deactivated it, moved the assets to DIA under operational control and retired the nickname. The board treats the retirement of a nickname as a documented behaviour rather than an inference, because the letter that does it is in the file.

Roles the board assigns it: research, concealment, custody, funding.

### Open questions on this node

- The command's 1977 establishment order has not been opened. The board's start date for this node is the year INSCOM's own memorandum gives for the start of the work, not for the command, and the record says so.
- The CENTER LANE security classification guide is on the board's source list with eight pages exempt. What those eight pages withhold is the single most direct statement available about what the access categories were protecting, and it has not been obtained.
- What DIA did with the assets and resources after 2 March 1985 is not established by anything opened for this node. The attachment is documented; the destination programme's own record is not.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 120 of 1159, score 57). 6 connections, 5 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A3/D2.

# 2000-2016: AAWSAP / AATIP

## DoDI 5205.11, Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD SAPs (12 Sep 2024)

DoDI 5205.11, Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD SAPs (12 Sep 2024) is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2013-02-06; axes C1 A3 D2; 7 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: dod-issuance.

### What it is

DoD Instruction 5205.11, 'Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD Special Access Programs (SAPs),' signed by the Deputy Secretary of Defense and effective February 6, 2013, cancelling the classified DoDI 0-5205.11 of July 1, 1997. It is the unclassified, publicly releasable rulebook for how the Department creates, nests, staffs, reports, waives reporting on, and disestablishes special access programs, and it implements the congressional oversight statute at 10 U.S.C. 119.

### Why it is implicated

This is the machine's operating manual, published in the open, and it describes every capability a concealed legacy programme would need. It defines the unacknowledged SAP, whose very existence is not acknowledged to anyone not cleared for it. It defines the waived SAP, for which the Secretary of Defense has set aside the reporting Congress imposed. It builds a four-level nesting hierarchy -- umbrella, compartment, sub-compartment, project -- and expressly contemplates unacknowledged waived sub-elements added underneath a non-waived umbrella, which is a documented mechanism for hiding a black effort inside a program Congress has already been told about. It authorises 'carve-out' proposals to relieve the Defense Security Service of industrial security oversight over contractor performance. And it conditions access not merely on need-to-know and clearance but on a determination that the individual 'will clearly and materially contribute to the execution or oversight of the program' -- the discretionary gate through which David Grusch's read-on request was denied.

### The longer account

The most useful thing about DoDI 5205.11 is that it is unclassified and approved for public release with unlimited releasability. The Department published its own concealment architecture and nobody read it.

Start with the vocabulary, because the Glossary is where the load is carried. An 'unacknowledged SAP' is 'A SAP having enhanced security measures ensuring the existence of the program is not acknowledged, affirmed, or made known to any person not authorized for such information.' A 'waived SAP' is 'A SAP for which the SecDef has waived applicable reporting ... following a determination of adverse effect to national security. An unacknowledged SAP that has more restrictive reporting and access controls than other unacknowledged SAPs.' A 'waiver' more generally is 'A decision that provides an approved exception to or deviation from a SAP security standard.' Two of the three definitions describe subtraction from the oversight baseline.

The waiver traces to statute. 10 U.S.C. 119(e)(1) lets the Secretary of Defense waive any requirement that certain information be included in a SAP report where inclusion 'would adversely affect the national security,' case by case; 119(e)(2) routes the withheld content and the justification 'jointly to the chairman and ranking minority member of each of the defense committees.' Twelve members. No staff named. That is the entire congressional footprint of a waived SAP.

The governance layer is three committees stacked above the Deputy Secretary: the SAP Oversight Committee (SAPOC), the Senior Review Group (SRG), and the SAP Senior Working Group (SSWG), which 'advise and assist the SecDef and DepSecDef in the management, administration, and oversight of DoD SAPs.' The SRG 'Reviews all waived DoD SAPs annually' and provides recommendations to the SAPOC 'concerning continued waived status.' There is, in other words, an internal annual re-authorisation cycle for programmes Congress cannot see -- run entirely by the Department.

Establishment is the part that looks like oversight. The SAPOC reviews the package and submits it to the Deputy Secretary of Defense for decision. 'If approved, the DepSecDef will submit formal notification letters to Congress that the SAP has been established.' And: 'The SAP may not be initiated until the congressional defense committees are notified of the program and a period of 30 days has elapsed after such notification is received.' That mirrors 10 U.S.C. 119(f). The Instruction also fixes the annual reporting calendar: SAP Listing on February 1, SAP Annual Report on March 1, forwarded to the congressional defense committees by the DoD SAPCO. Those dates correspond to 10 U.S.C. 119(b) and 119(a) respectively -- and both of those statutory reporting requirements were terminated effective December 31, 2021 by section 1061 of Public Law 114-328. The Instruction's reporting calendar survives on paper; the statutory obligation behind it does not.

Now the nesting. Enclosure 5 sets out the SAP hierarchy: a SAP is called an 'umbrella' if it has subordinate elements, which in descending order are compartments, sub-compartments, and projects. A person accessed to a compartment is also accessed to all subordinate elements 'unless an exception is specifically approved by the CA SAPCO.' Subordinate elements 'will be presumed to be within the same type unless' otherwise designated -- and the Instruction expressly addresses the case of unacknowledged waived sub-elements added under a non-waived umbrella, requiring specific SAPCO action. Read that as an analyst: the doctrine anticipates, and provides procedure for, burying a waived unacknowledged effort inside a programme that has been properly reported. This is the documented, unclassified mechanism corresponding exactly to Grusch's charge of 'nesting UAP activities in conventional SAP/CAP programs ... without appropriate reporting to various oversight authorities.'

Enclosure 6 lists what a Cognizant Authority SAPCO must notify the Director, DoD SAPCO of in writing. The list is a taxonomy of the moves available: change of category (acquisition, intelligence, operations and support); 'Change of type (acknowledged, unacknowledged)'; 'Alteration of scope'; IJSTO apportionment or deapportionment; disestablishment by termination, cancellation or transition; 'Support of non-DoD SAPs'; 'Addition, deletion, or changes of compartments, sub-compartments, and projects'; 'Proposals to relieve DSS of NISP security oversight responsibilities (carve-out) when required'; 'Change of nickname, codeword, PID'; and 'Foreign involvement.' Every one of these is an entry point for an analyst. Nickname and codeword changes break longitudinal tracking. Carve-outs remove the Defense Security Service from contractor facilities. 'Support of non-DoD SAPs' is the doctrinal bridge to DHS, DOE and IC compartments -- the category KONA BLUE would have occupied had it been approved.

Access is the human gate. 'Access to a SAP will be strictly limited to the minimum number of personnel necessary for execution of the program. Granting access to a SAP will be based solely upon a determination that the individual has a valid NTK, has the requisite security clearance, meets approved personnel prerequisites, and will clearly and materially contribute to the execution or oversight of the program.' The final clause is a subjective judgment reserved to the programme, and it is the clause that answers Grusch's testimony that he was 'denied access to those additional read-ons when I requested it.' Under this Instruction, a person conducting oversight can be lawfully excluded on the programme's own determination that he would not materially contribute to it.

Two further details reward attention. Sample SAP approval documentation reproduced in the Instruction's appendices uses a notional unacknowledged operations-and-support SAP designated GTE, with planned unacknowledged sub-compartments including LEAF BARK (LBK) and a recommendation line reading 'That you approve GTE as an unacknowledged SAP and authorize' -- a published template showing exactly how a sub-compartment request is written and approved. And the Instruction carries its own expiry: it 'Must be reissued, cancelled, or certified current within 5 years of its publication ... If not it will expire effective February 6, 2023.'

### What it has denied

None. DoDI 5205.11 asserts nothing about UAP and denies nothing; it is a procedural issuance published with unlimited releasability. Its evidentiary value is that the Department has openly documented, in its own doctrine, every structural capability -- unacknowledged type, waived reporting, nested waived sub-elements under reported umbrellas, industrial-security carve-outs, and discretionary denial of oversight read-ons -- that a concealed legacy programme would require.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, funding, oversight, custody.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether DoDI 5205.11 was reissued, cancelled or certified current before its stated February 6, 2023 expiry, and what issuance governs DoD SAPs now.
- How many DoD SAPs are currently designated waived, and whether that number has ever been disclosed even to the twelve members who receive 119(e) material.
- Whether any unacknowledged waived sub-element has ever been added under a non-waived umbrella in a manner that kept it out of the congressional notification stream, and what the SAPCO record of such additions shows.
- Which contractor facilities currently operate under NISP carve-outs, and who inspects them.
- Whether the SAP Listing and SAP Annual Report continued to be produced after the December 31, 2021 statutory termination, and if so whether they are still transmitted to Congress.
- Whether GTE and LEAF BARK are purely notional or were drawn from real program nomenclature for the published sample.
- Whether the SAPOC, SRG or SSWG has ever reviewed a programme relating to materials of unknown origin, and whether their minutes exist as records subject to section 1842 of the FY2024 NDAA.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 58 of 1159, score 75). 7 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A3/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** A SAP disestablishment plan must address contracting, fiscal, audit, property disposition, classified material disposition, training, public affairs, legal, logistics and technical actions, and the CA SAPCO must provide the termination plan to the DoD SAPCO for SSWG review of potential impact on other programs. Source: DoDI 5205.11, Enclosure 6, paragraph 4.a, https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Access is conditioned on a determination that the individual 'has a valid NTK, has the requisite security clearance, meets approved personnel prerequisites, and will clearly and materially contribute to the execution or oversight of the program' -- a discretionary gate held by the programme. Source: DoDI 5205.11, section 5.b, https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** BUDGET PAPER TRAIL. Para 7.2.h. (congressional budget justification materials): waived program justification material goes only to committee staff directors, not to the committees as bodies. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Cognizant Authority SAPCOs must notify the Director, DoD SAPCO in writing of proposals for change of category; 'Change of type (acknowledged, unacknowledged)'; 'Alteration of scope'; IJSTO apportionment or deapportionment; disestablishment (termination, cancellation, transition); 'Support of non-DoD SAPs'; addition, deletion or changes of compartments, sub-compartments and projects; 'Proposals to relieve DSS of NISP security oversight responsibilities (carve-out) when required'; 'Change of nickname, codeword, PID'; and 'Foreign involvement.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, Enclosure 6, paragraph 2.a, https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoD Instruction 5205.11, 'Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD Special Access Programs (SAPs),' is effective February 6, 2013, is signed by the Deputy Secretary of Defense, cancels the classified DoDI 0-5205.11 of July 1, 1997, and carries releasability 'Unlimited.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, February 6, 2013, sections 7-8 and Enclosure 1 reference (a), https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf

- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 Enclosure 2 requires the Director, DoD SAPCO to establish 'policies and applicable procedures to satisfy reporting requirements for DSS carve-outs' and to provide 'DSS confirmation of SAP facilities where the DSS has been carved out of security oversight functions.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, Enclosure 2, para 2.k
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 Enclosure 6 PSAP timeline: 'NLT P+180 - DepSecDef decision. Congressional notification or PSAP termination as appropriate. NLT P+210 - End of 30-day period without Congressional decision.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, Appendix 1 to Enclosure 6, Figure 2
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 Enclosure 7 requires each SAP record file to include 'Written approval by SecDef or DepSecDef of carve-out status when DSS is relieved of industrial security oversight responsibilities' and 'The identification of SAP facilities (or identification of the SAPCO-approved database that contains the information) that have been carved-out based upon this authority.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, Enclosure 7, para 1.a
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 Glossary: 'waived SAP. An unacknowledged SAP that has more restrictive reporting and access controls than other unacknowledged SAPs.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, Glossary Part II
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 SAP Approval Package Checklist item 2: 'Type, Category, and Classification of SAP. If unacknowledged and reporting requirements are to be waived, attach justification to waive the requirements of Volumes 2A and 2B of DoD 7000.14-R.' - **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 para 5.d requires every DoD SAP to be assigned an unclassified nickname generated under CJCSM 3150.29D, and provides that 'Unclassified nicknames will be used in the annual SAP report to Congress.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, para 5.d
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 sample action memo recommendation: 'That you approve GTE as an unacknowledged SAP and authorize carve-out status and the mandatory administration of CI polygraphs, and that you sign the notification letters to Congress (TAB C) identifying GREEN TREE as a new SAP.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, Appendix 3 to Enclosure 6, Figure 4
- **Tier 1 documented.** GAO ACCESS IS GATED. Para 7.2.g.: authorized access approval authorities may grant SAP access to GAO staff for specific projects only after the Director, DoD SAPCO consults with the chairman and ranking defense committee members. Congress's own audit arm cannot reach a DoD SAP without the audited department's SAPCO first clearing it with four members. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Header: DoD Instruction 5205.11, 'Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD Special Access Programs'; effective September 12, 2024; reissues and cancels DoDI 5205.11, February 6, 2013, as amended; also incorporates and cancels the Deputy Secretary of Defense Memorandum, 'Integrated Joint Special Technical Operations (IJSTO) Policy', April 5, 2021; approved by Kathleen H. Hicks, Deputy Secretary of Defense. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** PERIODIC REVIEW OF WAIVED STATUS EXISTS. Para 3.5.a.(3)(d) makes the SAPOC annual review cover 'All waived DoD SAPs to determine the need for continued waived status,' as part of the 3.5.a.(3) mandate to 'Conduct an annual review of DoD SAPs to assess the continuing need for protection of CPI within SAP security controls, in accordance with E.O. 13526 and DoDD 5205.07.' Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Para 4.1.a.(2): 'Within 45 calendar days of creation of the SAP project, the CA will submit' the required documentation, establishing an internal 45-day paper deadline for new SAP projects, distinct from the statutory 30-day congressional pre-initiation notice under 10 U.S.C. 119(f). Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Para 5.1.e.(1): 'The use of a polygraph examination as a mandatory access determination requirement must be approved by the Secretary of Defense.' Mandatory-polygraph SAP access is a Secretary-level decision, which makes the presence of a polygraph requirement a marker of a program that reached the top of the department. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Para 7.1.: 'All DoD employees accessed to DoD SAPs will coordinate with and notify the DoD and OA SAPCOs through the CA SAPCO when intending to brief or provide DoD SAP material to any appropriately accessed members of Congress or professional staff member.' A cleared DoD employee cannot lawfully brief even an accessed member of Congress without first routing notice through the SAP's own control office. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Para 7.2.f. verbatim: 'The personal staff of a member of Congress will not be granted access to DoD SAPs.' Non-committee members and staff may be granted access only to NON-waived SAPs, and only 'by the Director, DoD SAPCO following consultation with the respective chamber' (7.2.d.). Senate and House leadership may each nominate one professional staff member for access to all DoD SAPs (7.2.e.). Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sample SAP approval documentation reproduced in the Instruction uses a notional unacknowledged operations-and-support SAP designated GTE with planned unacknowledged sub-compartments including LEAF BARK (LBK), and a recommendation line reading 'That you approve GTE as an unacknowledged SAP and authorize.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, Enclosure 6 appendices (sample SAP approval memorandum), https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** TIER ARCHITECTURE. DoDI 5205.11 establishes a tiered SAP structure: umbrellas at tier 1, compartments at tier 2, sub-compartments at tier 3 and below; only the Secretary or Deputy Secretary of Defense may alter tier 1 structures. It distinguishes 'structural SAP: A SAP that provides organization to the architecture and does not protect discrete CPI' from 'content SAP: A SAP that protects discrete CPI.' Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Instruction contains a self-expiry clause: it 'Must be reissued, cancelled, or certified current within 5 years of its publication in accordance with DoDI 5025.01. If not it will expire effective February 6, 2023 and be removed from the DoD Issuances Website.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, section 8.b, https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Instruction defines a waived SAP as 'A SAP for which the SecDef has waived applicable reporting ... following a determination of adverse effect to national security. An unacknowledged SAP that has more restrictive reporting and access controls than other unacknowledged SAPs.' Source: DoDI 5205.11, Glossary, https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodi/520511p.pdf

### Its strongest connections

- DoDI 5205.11, Management, Administration, and Oversight of DoD SAPs (12 Sep 2024) runs out to **Waived Unacknowledged SAP (waived USAP), the mechanism** (program, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: para 1.2.f designates select unacknowledged SAPs as waived. The statute permits the waiver; this Instruction is where the Department operates it. Para 1.2.f. is DoD's own designation authority - the Secretary 'may designate select unacknowledged SAPs as "waived"' - and Enclosure 3 tasks the Senior Review Group with reviewing 'all waived DoD SAPs' annually and advising the SAPOC. Para 7.2.a.(3) then sets who is told, and it is wider than the statute's twelve: the chair, the ranking member, the staff directors and one designated security manager of the defense AND intelligence committees. DoDI 5205.11 (effective 12 September 2024) sets out the prospective SAP approval sequence in days from establishment, the review bodies (SSWG, Senior Review Group, SAPOC) and the oversight roles. # 2017-2026: Post-disclosure

## House Oversight hearing, 26 July 2023 - 'Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena: Implications on National Security, Public Safety, and Government Transparency'

House Oversight hearing, 26 July 2023 - 'Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena: Implications on National Security, Public Safety, and Government Transparency' is a event on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2023; axes C2 A2 D3; 20 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: congressional-hearings.

### What it is

A hearing of the House Committee on Oversight and Accountability's Subcommittee on National Security, the Border, and Foreign Affairs, held 26 July 2023 at 10:06 a.m. in room 2154 of the Rayburn House Office Building, chaired by Rep. Glenn Grothman. The witnesses were Ryan Graves, David Grusch and retired Commander David Fravor. It was the first congressional hearing at which a former intelligence officer testified under oath that the United States operates a multi-decade crash retrieval and reverse-engineering programme outside congressional oversight.

### Why it is implicated

This hearing converted the legacy programme allegation from journalism into sworn testimony before a committee of jurisdiction, and it did so with three specific mechanisms attached: how the programme is hidden (special access programs the witness was denied read-on to), how it is paid for (misappropriation of funds and self-funding), and who holds the material (contractors, described by the witness as 'the metal benders', who are under no requirement to report UAP). Fravor's written statement establishes that the most corroborated modern military encounter was never investigated and the tapes were never collected - an evidentiary vacuum that is itself the artefact. The hearing also demonstrated the ceiling on congressional capability: members were denied a SCIF, no contractor was called, no document production was compelled, and every substantive answer was deferred to a closed session that has never been publicly confirmed to have occurred on these terms. The committee's own wrap-up release then framed the day around pilot reporting and stigma, omitting the crash retrieval testimony entirely.

### The longer account

The subcommittee met at 10:06 a.m. on 26 July 2023 in 2154 Rayburn. Present were Representatives Grothman, Gosar, Foxx, Sessions, Biggs, Mace, LaTurner, Armstrong, Perry, Garcia, Moskowitz and Frost; also present, waived on, were Luna, Burlison, Gaetz, Burchett, Comer, Ogles, Langworthy, Raskin and Ocasio-Cortez. The unusual size of the 'also present' list is the first fact about this hearing: members from outside the subcommittee, from both parties, came to sit in.

The second fact came in Burchett's opening. Members, he said, 'were even denied access to a classified briefing in a SCIF prior to this hearing due to the amount of hoops that we had to jump through to grant temporary clearance to witness Grusch, who has knowledge of classified information.' The committee therefore held a public hearing about compartmented programmes without the means to hear about compartmented programmes. Every subsequent deferral in the transcript traces back to that.

Grusch's written opening statement is the core document. He identifies himself as an intelligence officer of fourteen years, a USAF major, and from 2021 to 2023 a GS-15 at the National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency, where he was his agency's co-lead for UAP and transmedium object analysis reporting to the UAP Task Force and then to AARO. He states that he became a whistleblower 'through a PPD-19 Urgent Concern filing with the Intelligence Community Inspector General (ICIG)'. He describes a specific tasking: 'In 2019, the UAPTF director tasked me to identify all Special Access Programs & Controlled Access Programs (SAPs/CAPs) we needed to satisfy our congressionally mandated mission.' And then the sentence the hearing is remembered for: 'I was informed, in the course of my official duties, of a multi-decade UAP crash retrieval and reverse engineering program to which I was denied access to those additional read-on's.' His closing statement calls the programmes he reported the 'Non-Human Reverse Engineering Programs' and predicts their revelation would act as 'an ontological (earth-shattering) shock'.

Under questioning the mechanism claims sharpened. Asked by Rep. Moskowitz how such a programme gets funded, Grusch answered: 'I will give you generalities. I can get very specific in a closed session. But misappropriation of funds and self-fund.' Asked whether that meant money budgeted for one programme goes elsewhere, he said 'Yes. I have specific knowledge of that. Yes.' Asked by Rep. Ocasio-Cortez about interactions between defence contractors and UAP activities, he described 'the contractors, you know, or the metal benders, so to speak, the ones actually doing specific performance on government contracts.' Asked whether those contractors are required to report UAP sightings, he answered: 'In terms of the contractors? Not that I am aware of.' That single exchange is the cleanest statement on the record of the regulatory gap that would allow contractor custody to persist without disclosure.

On where and who, he refused in open session and said the information had gone elsewhere: 'I cannot discuss that publicly, but I did provide that information both to the intel committees and the Inspector General.' Asked by Burchett whether it could be given in a SCIF, he replied 'Sure, if you had the appropriate accesses.' Asked when the government first became aware, he said: 'I like to use the term nonhuman. I do not like to denote origin. Keeps the aperture open both scientifically. Certainly, like I have discussed publicly, previously 1930's.' Asked by Rep. Burlison whether he had personally seen the craft or bodies, he distinguished carefully: on the craft, 'I have to be careful to describe what I have seen firsthand and not in this environment'; on bodies, 'That is something I have not witnessed myself.' The witness is testifying to what he was told and what he documented, not to personal observation, and the transcript is unambiguous that he drew that line himself.

Fravor's written statement supplies the other half. He commanded VFA-41 aboard USS Nimitz in November 2004 and describes the Princeton having tracked the objects on Aegis 'for the previous 2 weeks', descending from above 80,000 feet to 20,000 feet, staying for hours, and going 'straight back up'. His closing judgement is about oversight, not phenomenology: 'even Unacknowledged Waived programs have some level of oversight by the appropriate committee members in the House and Senate and this work that is said to be occurring from Whistleblower testimonies should not be exempt.' And the line that indicts the system: 'What is shocking is that the incident was never investigated, none of my crew were ever questioned, tapes were never taken, and after a couple of days, it turned into a great story to tell friends.'

The official denial was read into the record by a member reciting AARO director Sean Kirkpatrick's prior testimony that there has been 'no credible evidence thus far of extraterrestrial activity' or of 'off-world technology brought to the attention of the office' - a formulation that is a statement about what reached one office, not about what exists.

What the hearing did not produce is as important as what it did. No contractor was called. No document was subpoenaed. The Committee's own wrap-up release, published the same day, lists its Key Takeaways as poor reporting processes and retaliation against pilots, and quotes Graves estimating that only 'somewhere near five percent' of pilot sightings are reported. Crash retrieval, non-human craft and contractor custody do not appear in the committee's summary of its own hearing.

### What it has denied

The Department of Defense's position at the hearing was represented only indirectly, through a member quoting AARO director Sean Kirkpatrick's prior testimony that there is 'no credible evidence thus far of extraterrestrial activity' and no 'off-world technology brought to the attention of the office'. No DoD or AARO witness appeared, and the Department made no on-the-record response to the funding-diversion or contractor-custody testimony.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure, concealment, funding.

### Open questions on this node

- Grusch stated he provided names, locations and documentation to the ICIG and the intelligence committees; none of that material has been published or acknowledged as received in any public committee document.
- Whether the classified session Grusch repeatedly offered was ever held on the terms he described, and by which committee.
- Which Special Access Programs Grusch identified in his 2019 UAPTF tasking, and whether that tasking product still exists as a retrievable document.
- The publicly retrievable copies of the printed record (CHRG-118hhrg53022) and the committee transcript PDF both truncate before the closing rounds of questioning; a complete verified text of the final third of the hearing has not been obtained for this dossier.
- No contractor named in open or closed session has been publicly identified, and no committee has issued a subpoena arising from this hearing.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 24 of 1159, score 103). 25 connections, 3 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C2/A2/D3.
