## FY2024 NDAA, Public Law 118-31 - Div. A, Title XVIII, Subtitle C (secs. 1841-1843), UAP Records Collection; Div. G (IAA FY2024) secs. 7341, 7343

FY2024 NDAA, Public Law 118-31 - Div. A, Title XVIII, Subtitle C (secs. 1841-1843), UAP Records Collection; Div. G (IAA FY2024) secs. 7341, 7343 is a legislation on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2023; axes C2 A3 D3; 12 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: authorizing-statutes.

### What it is

The National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2024, H.R. 2670, became Public Law 118-31 on December 22, 2023. It contains four UAP-related provisions: section 1687 (funding cutoff for unreported UAP special access programs), sections 1841-1843 (the Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Records Collection at the National Archives), section 7341 (a reporting-channel adjustment) and section 7343 (the parallel Intelligence Authorization funding cutoff). It is what survived of the Schumer-Rounds UAP Disclosure Act after House-Senate conference.

### Why it is implicated

The enacted law is simultaneously the strongest and the weakest document on this board. Strongest, because the Joint Explanatory Statement of the House-Senate conference committee states in the official legislative history that the section 1687 funding prohibition "could cover" activities including "Analyzing such craft, or pieces or components thereof," "Actions relating to reverse engineering or replicating unidentified anomalous phenomena technology or performance," propulsion "based on or derived from or inspired by inspection, analysis, or reverse engineering of recovered unidentified anomalous phenomena craft or materials," and "Any aerospace craft that uses propulsion technology other than chemical propellants, solar power, and electric ion thrust." Conferees do not write six itemized categories of a thing that does not exist. Weakest, because everything with teeth was removed: the same JES records that "The agreement does not include the provisions that would establish an independent Review Board, a Review Board staff, eminent domain authority, or a controlled disclosure process," and section 1843 hands the postponement decision back to "the original classification authority", the same officials the Senate bill was designed to review. The difference between the Senate text and the enacted text is the measurable output of the concealment effort in a single legislative cycle.

### The longer account

The FY2024 NDAA is a 973-page enrolled bill authorizing $886 billion. The Senate approved the conference version 87-13 on 13 December 2023, the House 310-118 the next day, and the President signed it on 22 December.

Section 1687, "Limitation on Use of Funds for Certain Unreported Programs," is the money clause. Subsection (a): "None of the funds authorized to be appropriated or otherwise made available by this Act may be obligated or expended in support of any activities involving unidentified anomalous phenomena protected under any form of special access or restricted access limitations unless the Secretary of Defense has provided the details of the activity to the appropriate congressional committees and congressional leadership, including for any activities described in a report released by the All-Domain Anomaly Resolution Office in fiscal year 2024." Subsection (b) closes the contractor channel: citing DoD Instruction 3204.01, it provides that independent research and development funding relating to UAP "shall not be allowable as indirect expenses for purposes of contracts covered by such instruction, unless such material and information is made available the appropriate congressional committees and congressional leadership." The missing preposition is in the enacted text. Subsection (c) defines the appropriate committees as the congressional defense committees plus HPSCI and SSCI, and congressional leadership as the four leaders.

That IR&D subsection is the sleeper. It is a legislative acknowledgment that UAP-related work might sit inside contractor overhead accounts rather than a government program element, funded by the government as an allowable indirect cost without ever appearing as a DoD program. Congress does not write a prohibition against a billing practice nobody uses.

The Joint Explanatory Statement explains what the conferees did to the Senate provision (formerly section 1646): they removed the direction to provide information to AARO, and summarized the prohibition. Then they wrote six numbered categories of activity the prohibition "could cover": recruiting, employing, equipping and providing security for government or contractor personnel with a mission of "capturing, recovering, and securing unidentified anomalous phenomena craft or pieces and components of such craft"; analyzing such craft or components "including for the purpose of determining properties, material composition, method of manufacture, origin, characteristics, usage and application, performance, operational modalities, or reverse engineering of such craft or component technology"; protecting UAP activities and information "from disclosure or compromise"; actions relating to reverse engineering or replicating UAP technology or performance; development of propulsion technology or aerospace craft "based on or derived from or inspired by" reverse engineering of recovered UAP craft or materials; and "Any aerospace craft that uses propulsion technology other than chemical propellants, solar power, and electric ion thrust." That sixth category is a definition by exclusion of every propulsion method the conferees could not name.

Section 7343 does the same on the Intelligence Authorization side, substituting the DNI for the Secretary of Defense and adding the Appropriations Committees. Its IR&D subsection contains a dangling cross-reference, "contracts covered by such instruction," with no instruction identified anywhere in the section. The intelligence committee chairmen entered an Explanatory Statement in the Congressional Record on 14 December 2023 saying section 7343 was intended "to avoid technology and security stovepipes and expand awareness regarding any historical exotic technology antecedents previously provided by the Federal Government for research and development purposes if they are shown to exist."

Sections 1841-1843 are the records regime. Section 1841 directs the Archivist, within 60 days of enactment, to commence establishing the "Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Records Collection," consisting of "record copies of all Government, Government-provided, or Government-funded records relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena, technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence (or equivalent subjects by any other name with the specific and sole exclusion of temporarily non-attributed objects)." Those three terms appear in enacted United States statute with no definitions attached, because the definitions lived in the Senate bill and were dropped. Congress legislated about non-human intelligence and then declined to say what it meant.

Section 1842 sets the mechanics. Each head of a government office had 300 days to review, identify and organize its UAP records; NARA fixed the operative date at 20 October 2024. No UAP record may be destroyed, altered or mutilated, and no record already publicly disclosed may be withheld, redacted, postponed or reclassified. Agencies must give precedence to records "that most unambiguously and definitively pertain to unidentified anomalous phenomena, technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence," to records under active FOIA litigation, and to records "with earliest provenance." Subsection (g)(2)(D) is the 25-year rule: each record must be disclosed in full not later than 25 years after creation unless the President certifies continued postponement is necessary because of "an identifiable harm to the military defense, intelligence operations, law enforcement, or conduct of foreign relations" of "such gravity that it outweighs the public interest in disclosure."

Section 1843 is where the Senate's design was inverted. Postponement is decided not by an independent board but by "the original classification authority," on "clear and convincing evidence" of one of four enumerated harms.

The JES at page 1330 states the outcome without euphemism: "The agreement does not include the provisions that would establish an independent Review Board, a Review Board staff, eminent domain authority, or a controlled disclosure process."

On 13 December 2023 Schumer told the Senate: "It is really an outrage that the House didn't work with us on adopting our proposal for a review board... Now it means that declassification of UAP records will be largely up to the same entities that blocked and obfuscated their disclosure for decades." He added that multiple credible sources had notified them that UAP information was being withheld from Congress, "which, if true, is a violation of the laws requiring full notification to the legislative branch." Nine days later the President signed the bill and issued a signing statement listing section 1687 among provisions requiring submission of "highly sensitive classified information" to Congress.

### What it has denied

The Presidential signing statement of December 22, 2023 is the operative denial artifact: it identified section 1687 as one of several provisions compelling delivery of highly sensitive classified information to Congress and reserved to the Executive a construction of those provisions consistent with its own view of national security, a reservation aimed squarely at the one UAP provision with a funding sanction attached.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, oversight, funding.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether any agency has ever certified compliance with section 1687, and whether any funds have been withheld or an activity terminated under it.
- Which agencies transmitted records to the UAP Records Collection by the October 20, 2024 deadline, in what volume, and how many records were postponed under section 1843 and reported under the 15-day notice rule.
- How many records have been withdrawn from the Collection under section 1843(b) by Senior Agency Officials, and on what basis.
- Whether the President has ever made a section 1842(g)(2)(D) certification to continue postponing a UAP record beyond 25 years.
- How agencies are interpreting the undefined statutory terms 'technologies of unknown origin,' 'non-human intelligence' and 'temporarily non-attributed objects' in their identification and review, and whether NARA issued any definitional guidance beyond the metadata template.
- Whether any contractor has been denied recovery of independent research and development indirect costs under section 1687(b) or section 7343(c), which would be a Defense Contract Audit Agency and Defense Contract Management Agency record.
- Who the 'multiple credible sources' were who told Schumer information had been withheld from Congress.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 41 of 1159, score 87). 12 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C2/A3/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Division G (Intelligence Authorization Act for FY2024), sec. 7343(b): 'None of the funds authorized to be appropriated or otherwise made available by this division may be obligated or expended in support of any activity involving unidentified anomalous phenomena protected under any form of special access or restricted access limitation unless the Director of National Intelligence has provided the details of the activity to the appropriate committees of Congress and congressional leadership, including for any activities described in a report released by the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office in fiscal year 2024.' Source: PL 118-31 sec. 7343(b), 137 Stat. 1064
- **Tier 1 documented.** In a colloquy with Senator Rounds on the Senate floor on December 13, 2023, Senator Schumer said: "It is really an outrage that the House didn't work with us on adopting our proposal for a review board, which, by definition, needs bipartisan consent. Now it means that declassification of UAP records will be largely up to the same entities that blocked and obfuscated their disclosure for decades," and in the same colloquy: "We have also been notified by multiple credible sources that information on UAPs has also been withheld from Congress, which, if true, is a violation of the laws requiring full notification to the legislative branch, especially as it relates to the four congressional leaders, Defense Committees, and the Intelligence Committee." Source: Cong. Rec., Dec. 13, 2023, pp. S5930-S5931; reproduced at https://douglasjohnson.ghost.io/uap-disclosure-act-pentagon-rewrite-nov-2023/
- **Tier 1 documented.** NARA created Record Group 615 and issued guidance to federal agencies on identification, preparation and transfer of UAP records pursuant to the statute. Source: National Archives UAP research topic page
- **Tier 1 documented.** PL 118-31 was 'Approved December 22, 2023.' Source: PLAW-118publ31 enactment line
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 1687 bars DoD funds for UAP activity under special or restricted access unless described to the congressional defense and intelligence committees and congressional leadership, and disallows UAP-related independent research and development as an indirect expense unless disclosed. Source: P.L. 118-31, Sec. 1687, GPO
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 1841 carries the note '44 USC 2107 note' and is titled 'UNIDENTIFIED ANOMALOUS PHENOMENA RECORDS COLLECTION AT THE NATIONAL ARCHIVES AND RECORDS ADMINISTRATION'. Sec. 1841(a)(1)(A) required the Archivist, within 60 days of enactment, to commence establishment of the 'Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Records Collection'. Source: PL 118-31 sec. 1841(a)(1)(A), 137 Stat. 699
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 1841 is noted as 44 U.S.C. 2107 note and required the Archivist to commence the collection within 60 days of enactment. Source: P.L. 118-31, Sec. 1841(a)(1)(A), GPO
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 1841 miscites its own definition, citing 'section 1673(n)(8) of the National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2022 (Public Law 117-81; 50 U.S.C. 3373)'; Sec. 1673 is a provision of the FY2023 NDAA, P.L. 117-263, codified at 50 U.S.C. 3373b. Source: P.L. 118-31, Sec. 1841(a)(1)(A), compared with P.L. 117-263, Sec. 1673, GPO
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 1841(a)(1)(C) defines the scope of the Collection: 'record copies of all Government, Government-provided, or Government-funded records relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena, technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence (or equivalent subjects by any other name with the specific and sole exclusion of temporarily non-attributed objects), which shall be transmitted to the National Archives in accordance with section 2107 of title 44, United States Code.' The phrases 'technologies of unknown origin' and 'non-human intelligence' are enacted federal statutory text. Source: PL 118-31 sec. 1841(a)(1)(C), 137 Stat. 699-700
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 1841(e) assigns continuing legislative oversight of the Collection to the Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs, Armed Services, and Select Intelligence Committees, and to the House Oversight and Accountability, Armed Services, and Permanent Select Intelligence Committees. Source: PL 118-31 sec. 1841(e), 137 Stat. 701
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 1842(a)(2) imposes absolute prohibitions: '(A) Destruction; alteration; mutilation.--No unidentified anomalous phenomena record shall be destroyed, altered, or mutilated in any way. (B) ... No unidentified anomalous phenomena record made available or disclosed to the public prior to the date of the enactment of this Act may be withheld, redacted, postponed for public disclosure, or reclassified. (C) ... No unidentified anomalous phenomena record created by a person or entity outside the Federal Government ... shall be withheld, redacted, postponed for public disclosure, or reclassified.' Source: PL 118-31 sec. 1842(a)(2), 137 Stat. 701
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 1842(c)(1) required each head of a Government office to review, identify and organise every UAP record in its custody not later than 300 days after enactment, a deadline of 17 October 2024. Source: P.L. 118-31, Sec. 1842(c)(1), GPO; enactment date 22 December 2023
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 1842(c)(1) required each head of a Government office, not later than 300 days after enactment (i.e. by approximately 16 October 2024), to 'review, identify, and organize each unidentified anomalous phenomena record in the custody or possession of the office for (A) disclosure to the public; and (B) transmission to the Archivist.' Source: PL 118-31 sec. 1842(c)(1), 137 Stat. 702
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 1842(g)(2)(D) sets the outer disclosure deadline: 'Each unidentified anomalous phenomena record shall be publicly disclosed in full, and available in the Collection, not later than the date that is 25 years after the date of the first creation of the record by the originating body, unless the President certifies that-- (i) continued postponement is made necessary by an identifiable harm to the military defense, intelligence operations, law enforcement, or conduct of foreign relations; and (ii) the identifiable harm is of such gravity that it outweighs the public interest in disclosure.' Source: PL 118-31 sec. 1842(g)(2)(D), 137 Stat. 704-705
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 1843(a) sets the postponement standard: disclosure 'may be postponed ... if the original classification authority makes a determination that there is clear and convincing evidence' of one of four harms, including that public disclosure would reveal 'any other matter currently relating to the military defense, intelligence operations, or conduct of foreign relations of the United States, the disclosure of which would demonstrably and substantially impair the national security of the United States'. The determination is made by the agency holding the record; no independent board reviews it. Source: PL 118-31 sec. 1843(a), 137 Stat. 705-706
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 1843(b) allows unilateral withdrawal: 'Senior Agency Officials designated in accordance with Executive Order 13526 or any successor Orders may withdraw records in the Collection that are determined to be both not related to unidentified anomalous phenomena and properly classified', subject only to notification of congressional leadership and the oversight committees named in sec. 1841(e). Source: PL 118-31 sec. 1843(b), 137 Stat. 706
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 7341 amends 50 U.S.C. 3373(k)(1) to shift the annual UAP report obligation from the DNI and SecDef jointly to 'the Director of the Office' (AARO). Source: PL 118-31 sec. 7341, 137 Stat. 1062-1063
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 7343(c): 'Independent research and development funding relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena shall not be allowable as indirect expenses for purposes of contracts covered by such instruction, unless such material and information is made available to the appropriate congressional committees and leadership.' This is a defense-contractor IR&D cost-allowability lever aimed at privately funded UAP work. Source: PL 118-31 sec. 7343(c), 137 Stat. 1064
- **Tier 1 documented.** Section 1687(a) of P.L. 118-31 provides: "None of the funds authorized to be appropriated or otherwise made available by this Act may be obligated or expended in support of any activities involving unidentified anomalous phenomena protected under any form of special access or restricted access limitations unless the Secretary of Defense has provided the details of the activity to the appropriate congressional committees and congressional leadership, including for any activities described in a report released by the All-Domain Anomaly Resolution Office in fiscal year 2024." Source: Pub. L. 118-31, sec. 1687(a) (Dec. 22, 2023), text at H. Rept. 118-301 pp. 489-490, https://douglasjohnson.ghost.io/content/files/2023/12/UAP-pages-only--final--from-NDAA--HR-2670--and-Joint-Explanatory-Statement-12-6-23.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Section 1687(b) provides that, consistent with DoD Instruction 3204.01 (August 20, 2014, incorporating change 2 dated July 9, 2020), "independent research and development funding relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena shall not be allowable as indirect expenses for purposes of contracts covered by such instruction, unless such material and information is made available the appropriate congressional committees and congressional leadership." Source: Pub. L. 118-31, sec. 1687(b), H. Rept. 118-301 p. 490
- **Tier 1 documented.** Section 1687(c) defines "appropriate congressional committees" as the congressional defense committees plus HPSCI and SSCI, and "congressional leadership" as the majority and minority leaders of the Senate and the Speaker and minority leader of the House. Source: Pub. L. 118-31, sec. 1687(c), H. Rept. 118-301 p. 490
- **Tier 1 documented.** Section 1841(a)(1)(C) provides that the Collection "shall consist of record copies of all Government, Government-provided, or Government-funded records relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena, technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence (or equivalent subjects by any other name with the specific and sole exclusion of temporarily non-attributed objects)." Source: Pub. L. 118-31, sec. 1841(a)(1)(C), H. Rept. 118-301 p. 568
- **Tier 1 documented.** Section 1842(c)(1) required each head of a Government office to review, identify and organize each UAP record in its custody within 300 days of enactment; NARA implemented this as a deadline of October 20, 2024, and stated it would accept only digital versions. Source: Pub. L. 118-31, sec. 1842(c)(1); https://www.archives.gov/records-mgmt/uap-guidance
- **Tier 1 documented.** Section 1842(g)(2)(D) requires each UAP record to be publicly disclosed in full not later than 25 years after its first creation "unless the President certifies that (i) continued postponement is made necessary by an identifiable harm to the military defense, intelligence operations, law enforcement, or conduct of foreign relations; and (ii) the identifiable harm is of such gravity that it outweighs the public interest in disclosure." Source: Pub. L. 118-31, sec. 1842(g)(2)(D), H. Rept. 118-301 pp. 573-574
- **Tier 1 documented.** Section 1843(a) vests the postponement determination in "the original classification authority" on a finding of "clear and convincing evidence" of one of four enumerated harms, restoring the decision to the classifying agency rather than the independent board the Senate had proposed; section 1843(b) permits Senior Agency Officials to withdraw records from the Collection determined to be both unrelated to UAP and properly classified on 60 days' advance notice to congressional leadership and the oversight committees, and section 1843(c) requires notice of any postponement within 15 days. Source: Pub. L. 118-31, sec. 1843, H. Rept. 118-301 pp. 574-575

## DoD Agency-Wide Financial Statement Audit (FY2018-present)

DoD Agency-Wide Financial Statement Audit (FY2018-present) is a program on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2018; axes C0 A1 D3; 9 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: Annual full-scope audit required by the CFO Act; disclaimer of opinion every year since inception.

### What it is

The Department of Defense's annual department-wide financial statement audit, first performed for fiscal year 2018 and repeated every year since, coordinated by the DoD Office of Inspector General across dozens of component audits. It has never produced a clean opinion. DoD is the only major federal agency that has never received an unmodified audit opinion on its financial statements, and its balance sheet is assembled through summary-level accounting adjustments, journal vouchers, that auditors have repeatedly found to be unsupported by documentation traceable to actual transactions.

### Why it is implicated

This is the financial-concealment thread and it requires no exotic assumption. If a department cannot demonstrate where more than four trillion dollars in reported assets sits, cannot reconcile its subsidiary systems to its general ledger, and closes the gap each quarter with trillions of dollars in undocumented journal vouchers, then the accounting system itself functions as a laundering mechanism whether or not anyone designed it that way. Money moved into an unacknowledged program does not need a fake line item; it needs only to enter an environment where the difference between the books and reality is routinely papered over at a scale that dwarfs the transaction. The DoD Inspector General documented $6.5 trillion in unsupported year-end adjustments and $2.8 trillion in unsupported third-quarter adjustments to the Army General Fund in a single fiscal year against an Army budget of roughly $120 billion. GAO has found that identified material weaknesses directly affect over half of DoD's reported assets. The audit failure is therefore not merely a governance embarrassment; it is the enabling condition for any funding stream the board alleges. It also functions as an oversight artifact with a deadline: Congress has now set a statutory requirement for a clean opinion by 31 December 2028, which for the first time gives concealment a dated, enforceable test to fail.

### The longer account

Start with the number that made the pattern famous. DoD Inspector General Report No. DODIG-2016-113, 'Army General Fund Adjustments Not Adequately Documented or Supported,' issued 26 July 2016, found that Army and Defense Finance and Accounting Service Indianapolis personnel did not adequately support $2.8 trillion in third-quarter adjustments and $6.5 trillion in year-end adjustments made to Army General Fund data during the fiscal year 2015 financial statement compilation. The Army's entire enacted budget that year was on the order of $120 billion. The unsupported year-end adjustments were therefore roughly fifty-four times the money Congress actually authorized the Army to spend.

The mechanism deserves precision, because the scale invites dismissal. A journal voucher is a summary-level accounting entry made when balances between two systems will not reconcile. It is legitimate when it is documented and tied to identifiable transactions. It is what auditors call a plug when it is not. DoD's feeder systems for procurement, logistics, personnel and property were never built to talk to its accounting systems, so at each reporting cycle the difference is closed by hand. The Inspector General's finding was not that the Army stole $6.5 trillion. It was that the Army could not demonstrate what the adjustments represented. From an audit standpoint those are functionally identical: unsupported entries at that magnitude mean the books cannot be used to detect anything, including diversion.

This was not new in 2016. Reuters correspondent Scot J. Paltrow's 2013 investigative series 'Unaccountable: The High Cost of the Pentagon's Bad Bookkeeping,' culminating in a special report published 18 November 2013, documented that fabricating entries to force agreement was standard operating procedure at DFAS offices handling the Army, Navy, Air Force and other defense agencies, and cited a December 2011 Pentagon Inspector General report finding that a single DFAS office in Columbus, Ohio had made at least $1.59 trillion in errors, including $538 billion in plugs, in fiscal year 2009 Air Force financial reports.

The modern audit era begins with fiscal year 2018, the first full-scope department-wide audit. It has failed every year since. For fiscal year 2024, GAO reported that DoD received a disclaimer of opinion for the seventh consecutive year, meaning DoD could not provide auditors sufficient, appropriate evidence to support the information in its financial statements, owing to ineffective systems and processes. DoD reported over $4.1 trillion in assets on its balance sheet as of 30 September 2024. That balance sheet consolidated information from sixty-seven separate DoD components. Eleven components received clean opinions, contributing $1.8 trillion of reported assets, 42.8 percent, and $3.1 trillion of reported liabilities, 72.2 percent. But GAO added the detail that guts the headline: nearly all of the assets attributable to clean-opinion components related to the Military Retirement Fund, a pension trust, while nearly all assets attributable to disclaimer components related to the Army, Navy and Air Force. The parts of DoD that can be audited are the parts that do not buy anything.

The DoD Office of Inspector General identified twenty-eight department-wide material weaknesses for fiscal year 2024. GAO's own analysis found that several of those DoD-wide material weaknesses directly affected $2.1 trillion of DoD's reported assets, 50.3 percent, and $146.9 billion of reported liabilities, 3.4 percent, and cautioned that pervasive material weaknesses may affect all balance sheet data. Congressional Research Service analysis of the FY2024 results adds that entities receiving disclaimers accounted for 44 percent of DoD's total assets and at least 68 percent of its total budgetary resources, and that DoD extended the target date for correcting its department-wide financial management systems modernization material weakness from FY2028 to FY2031, a slippage past the statutory audit deadline itself.

That deadline is the newest and most useful fact for the board. The National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2024 requires DoD to receive an unmodified audit opinion by 31 December 2028; CRS identifies the provision as Section 1005. GAO states the requirement flatly in GAO-25-108052. The department has therefore been given a legal obligation to become legible on a specific date, while simultaneously moving its own systems-modernization fix three years past that date.

The fiscal year 2025 cycle continued the pattern. The DoD Office of Inspector General issued Report No. DODIG-2026-032, 'Independent Auditor's Reports on the DoD FY 2025 Financial Statements,' comprising three separate audit reports addressing the financial statements, internal control over financial reporting, and compliance with laws and regulations. DoD received a disclaimer of opinion for the eighth consecutive time, on reported assets of approximately $4.65 trillion and reported liabilities of approximately $4.73 trillion, with results released 18 December 2025. After the seventh failure, the House Committee on Oversight and Government Reform publicly sought GAO engagement on waste, fraud and abuse at DoD.

What this means for the legacy-program argument is narrow and strong. It does not prove that money was diverted. It proves that DoD's accounting environment cannot rule out diversion at any scale below the noise floor, and that the noise floor is measured in trillions. Every other funding hypothesis on the board, whether contractor-held Independent Research and Development recovery, unacknowledged Special Access Programs, waived SAPs briefed only to a handful of members, or overhead absorption on classified contracts, sits downstream of one structural fact: the Department of Defense's books do not close, have never closed, and the entries that make them appear to close are, by the Inspector General's own finding, undocumented. Set beside STAR GATE, which consumed roughly $20 million across two decades, the scale mismatch is the whole argument. A real, funded, denied program of that size would not register as a rounding error in a single quarterly journal voucher.

### What it has denied

DoD does not deny the audit failures; it reframes them as a modernization timeline. The department's public posture is that disclaimers reflect systems and process immaturity rather than any inability to account for money, and that a clean opinion is achievable by the statutory 2028 deadline, while it simultaneously slipped its own financial-systems modernization remediation target from FY2028 to FY2031.

Roles the board assigns it: funding, concealment, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- Do unsupported journal vouchers at DFAS net to zero across the department, or is there a residual, and has any auditor ever attempted to compute it?
- How are Special Access Program and waived SAP obligations represented on the audited balance sheet, and are they carried at component level or aggregated in a way that defeats reconciliation?
- Which of the 28 FY2024 DoD-wide material weaknesses, if any, specifically implicate classified or restricted-access accounting environments auditors cannot enter?
- Have auditors ever been denied access to records on classification grounds during a DoD financial statement audit, and is that denial documented as a scope limitation anywhere?
- What is the disposition of contractor-held Independent Research and Development recovery on classified contracts, and does it appear anywhere in the audited statements?
- Does slipping the FM systems modernization target from FY2028 to FY2031 constitute a knowing statement that the Section 1005 deadline will not be met, and has any committee put that question on the record?
- Which specific components make up the 44 percent of assets and 68 percent of budgetary resources sitting under disclaimers, and what do those components buy?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 299 of 1159, score 39). 9 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A1/D3.

- DoD Agency-Wide Financial Statement Audit (FY2018-present) runs out to **Funding Channels** (domain, board tier A), a funding link at Tier 1 documented. The Department has undergone full-scope financial statement audits since FY2018 and has received a disclaimer of opinion every year through FY2025 - eight in a row. A disclaimer means the auditors could not obtain sufficient appropriate evidence to form an opinion, which is the auditable fact that defines the boundary of what can be traced from appropriation to expenditure. This is the single hardest, least deniable member of this domain. ## FY2024 NDAA sec. 1687, Limitation on Use of Funds for Certain Unreported Programs

FY2024 NDAA sec. 1687, Limitation on Use of Funds for Certain Unreported Programs is a legislation on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2023-12-22; axes C1 A3 D3; 9 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: statute.

### What it is

Section 1687 of the National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2024, Pub. L. 118-31, 137 Stat. 621, titled 'Limitation on Use of Funds for Certain Unreported Programs'. It has three subsections. (a) bars obligation or expenditure of funds authorised by the Act on any UAP-related activity 'protected under any form of special access or restricted access limitation' that has not been reported to Congress. (b), carrying the codification note '50 USC 3373 note', makes UAP-related independent research and development unallowable as an indirect expense under contracts covered by DoD Instruction 3204.01. (c) defines 'appropriate congressional committees' as the congressional defense committees plus HPSCI and SSCI, and imports the 50 U.S.C. 3373(n) definition of 'unidentified anomalous phenomena'.

### Why it is implicated

This is the point where the SAP architecture and the UAP question meet in enacted law rather than in testimony. Congress does not write a targeted funding prohibition, or a targeted bar on a specific category of contractor overhead recovery, for a subject matter it believes is absent from the portfolio. The provision is the legislature acting on the possibility that UAP work was being carried inside special access programmes it had not been told about, and inside contractor IR&D it was indirectly paying for. Its companion, section 7343 of the FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act, imposes the same limits on intelligence funds through the DNI rather than the Secretary of Defense, so the pair covers both sides of the DoD/IC seam.

### The longer account

Section 1687 does not repeal or amend 10 U.S.C. 119(e). It leaves the waiver lawful and operates on the money instead: the waiver may still be exercised, but funds authorised by this Act cannot be spent on a UAP-related special access activity that Congress has not been told about. That is a materially different instrument from an oversight reform, and it is the instrument Congress reached for.

The two subsections have different reach and it matters. Subsection (a) is limited to 'funds authorized to be appropriated or otherwise made available by this Act', so it expires with the fiscal year. Subsection (b), the IR&D allowability bar, carries no fiscal year limitation at all and sits as a note to 50 U.S.C. 3373. One was a one-year appropriations rider; the other was meant to persist.

Neither the FY2025 NDAA (P.L. 118-159) nor the FY2026 NDAA (P.L. 119-60) re-enacted the 1687(a) prohibition, which is recorded on this board as a full-text search finding rather than an inference.

### What it has denied

No denial attaches to the statute. The informative absence is that no DoD component has publicly identified a UAP-related special access programme in any report made under it, and that neither the FY2025 nor the FY2026 NDAA re-enacted the subsection (a) prohibition.

Roles the board assigns it: funding, oversight, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether any programme was actually reported to the appropriate congressional committees under 1687(a), and whether any funding was withheld as a result.
- Whether subsection (b)'s IR&D allowability bar has ever been applied to a specific contract, and by which contracting officer.
- Why neither the FY2025 nor the FY2026 NDAA re-enacted subsection (a), and whether anything replaced it.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 45 of 1159, score 84). 11 connections, 3 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A3/D3.

The link reads: Only the funding limitation and the IR&D restriction survived into law. Sec. 1687 and Sec. 7343 of Public Law 118-31 carry forward the funding cutoff and the IR&D clause from the Senate provision; the contractor disclosure, inventory and immunity subsections were removed. ## FY2026 NDAA, Public Law 119-60 - secs. 1671-1673, NORAD/NORTHCOM intercept briefings and UAP classification-guide accounting

FY2026 NDAA, Public Law 119-60 - secs. 1671-1673, NORAD/NORTHCOM intercept briefings and UAP classification-guide accounting is a legislation on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2025; axes C1 A2 D1; 9 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: authorizing-statutes.

### What it is

The National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2026, S. 2296 of the 119th Congress, signed into law on 18 December 2025 as Public Law 119-60. It carries three UAP-related provisions affecting AARO. It does not carry the UAP Disclosure Act, which was excluded for the third consecutive year.

### Why it is implicated

The FY2026 NDAA is the clearest available measurement of what Congress can and cannot pass on this subject. What survived is procedural: briefings, an inventory of classification guides, and reporting consolidation. What died, again, was every provision with teeth: records preservation, an independent Review Board, eminent domain over privately held recovered material, and mandatory declassification timelines. The pattern is now three cycles deep and consistent in kind: the disclosure architecture is introduced in the Senate with bipartisan leadership sponsorship, and it is removed in or around conference. One surviving provision is unusually revealing on its own terms, a directive that AARO account for the UAP-related security classification guides governing its own reports and investigations. A classification guide is a written instrument with an author, a date, and an original classification authority. Congress asking for an inventory of them is Congress conceding it does not know how many exist or who wrote them.

### The longer account

S. 2296 passed the Senate on 10 October 2025 and became P.L. 119-60 on 18 December 2025. Three UAP provisions made the conferenced text, all of them modifications to the existing AARO framework rather than new authorities.

The first amends the AARO mandate to require the director to supply Congress with briefings and data on UAP intercepts conducted by U.S. Northern Command and the North American Aerospace Defense Command going back to 2004, specifically the "number, location, and nature" of such intercepts, together with the procedures and protocols followed, "including any data collected or analyzed during such intercepts." The 2004 start date is not arbitrary; it is the year of the USS Nimitz/USS Princeton encounters off southern California. That a statutory obligation was needed in 2025 to get NORTHCOM and NORAD intercept data in front of Congress establishes that, for twenty-one years, the commands responsible for defending North American airspace were not routinely providing it, not to Congress, not to AARO, and not to the scientific community. Christopher Mellon, the former Deputy Assistant Secretary of Defense for Intelligence who now chairs the Disclosure Foundation's board, has made precisely that point publicly: the United States operates some of the world's most advanced sensor systems, and what those systems capture about anomalous incursions has not been reaching oversight.

The second provision directs AARO to account for the UAP-related security classification guides that apply to information used in reports and investigations of the phenomena. This is the provision an analyst should mine. Security classification guides are the operative documents of concealment: they are what tells a program office which facts are classified at what level and for how long, and they are signed by an original classification authority. Disclosure Foundation officials, Mellon among them, have argued in public that DoD classification guides have been applied inconsistently with executive order standards and have been used to suppress material that was previously unclassified. An accounting of those guides would produce a list of instruments, custodians and dates. Whether AARO produces it, and whether the accounting is delivered classified, is the next measurable event.

The third provision consolidates duplicative reporting requirements and streamlines how federal agencies provide data and information to the Pentagon for AARO's investigations. Streamlining is double-edged: it reduces friction for AARO, and it reduces the number of separate documents Congress receives.

Against that, consider what was excluded. Rep. Eric Burlison (R-MO) submitted the UAP Disclosure Act of 2025 as an amendment to the FY2026 NDAA in late August 2025. It would have prohibited the destruction or alteration of UAP records, created a UAP Records Collection at the National Archives, established an independent UAP Records Review Board, and required public disclosure of UAP records within twenty-five years absent a presidential certification of a clear national security reason for delay. It failed in the House. On the Senate side the same architecture was offered as S.Amdt. 3111 to S. 2296. Neither survived. The UAP Disclosure Act has now been excluded from three consecutive defense authorization bills despite bipartisan Senate backing associated with Schumer, Rounds and Gillibrand, and Liberation Times has reported that sources attribute the FY2026 failure specifically to Senator Mitch McConnell.

The records-destruction prohibition is the provision whose repeated exclusion should be read hardest. It is cheap, non-controversial on its face, and imposes no disclosure burden, it merely freezes the evidentiary record in place. Its removal, three cycles running, from a bill that Congress has enacted for more than six decades without fail, is a fact about the removers. The precedent for why it matters is documentary and sixty years old: GAO found in 1995 that the administrative records and outgoing message traffic of Roswell Army Air Field for the relevant period were destroyed with no indication of by whom or under what authority (see gao-nsiad-95-187). A statutory bar on destruction is the one measure that would prevent the 1995 finding from recurring, and it is the measure that keeps not passing.

Jordan Flowers, executive director of the Disclosure Foundation, the rebranded UAP Disclosure Fund, characterized the three enacted provisions as "a meaningful, if incremental, step toward transparency and oversight," and noted that two of them mirror recommendations Mellon and his team had promoted for years. That is the accurate reading: the FY2026 NDAA moved the oversight perimeter a few feet and left the custody question untouched.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, concealment, disclosure, funding.

### Open questions on this node

- What are the exact section numbers of the three UAP provisions in P.L. 119-60, and what deadlines and classification levels do they attach?
- Does the classification-guide accounting have to be delivered unclassified, or may it be delivered entirely in classified annex?
- Why 2004 as the start date for the NORTHCOM/NORAD intercept look-back, and who chose it?
- Did the FY2026 NDAA renew, allow to lapse, or silently supersede the FY2024 funding limitation on UAP-related special access programs?
- Who specifically moved to strike the records-destruction prohibition in conference, and is there a written record of the objection?
- Has AARO produced any deliverable under any of the three provisions as of the date of this dossier?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 91 of 1159, score 66). 9 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C1/A2/D1.

## FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act sec. 7343, Funding Limitations Relating to UAP

FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act sec. 7343, Funding Limitations Relating to UAP is a legislation on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2023-12-22; axes C1 A3 D3; 6 connections; in the funding band.

In one line: statute.

### What it is

Section 7343 of the Intelligence Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2024, enacted as division G of Pub. L. 118-31, 137 Stat. 1064, titled 'Funding Limitations Relating to Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena'. Subsection (b) bars obligation or expenditure of funds authorised by that division 'in support of any activity involving unidentified anomalous phenomena protected under any form of special access or restricted access limitation' that has not been reported to Congress. Subsection (c) makes UAP-related independent research and development unallowable as an indirect expense. Subsection (a)(1) defines 'appropriate committees of Congress' as SSCI, SASC and Senate Appropriations, plus HPSCI, HASC and House Appropriations; (a)(2) adds the four congressional leaders.

### Why it is implicated

7343 is the intelligence-community half of a matched pair. NDAA sec. 1687 imposes the same two limitations on defence funds through the Secretary of Defense; 7343 imposes them on intelligence funds through the Director of National Intelligence. Congress wrote the prohibition twice because the money and the authority run through two different chains, and a limitation binding only one of them would leave the other open. That is a legislative judgement about where UAP work could be hidden, expressed in enacted law rather than in testimony.

It is also the surviving fragment of something larger. 7343 is the truncated successor of sec. 1102 of the Senate-passed IAA division of S. 2226, which carried a contractor turn-in obligation, a list of non-earth-origin materials and a liability provision. None of that survived to enactment.

### The longer account

The pairing is the point. Section 1687 addresses the Secretary of Defense and binds funds authorised by the NDAA; section 7343 addresses the Director of National Intelligence and binds funds authorised by the intelligence division. Read against 50 U.S.C. 3348(g)(2)(B), which excludes the Department of Defense, the NNSA and IC agencies from the general SAP-reporting statute and routes each to its own regime, the pair looks less like duplication and more like Congress closing both sides of a seam it had itself created.

Neither text cites the other, and 3348 makes no reference to 7343. That was checked directly against the statutory text on 22 August 2026 rather than assumed, which is why the structural edges from this node are graded Tier 3.

### What it has denied

No denial attaches to the section. The informative absence is that no intelligence element has publicly identified a UAP-related programme reported under it, and that the turn-in obligation which would have forced the question out of the reporting frame and into physical custody was removed before enactment.

Roles the board assigns it: funding, oversight, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether any intelligence element has reported a UAP-related special or restricted access activity under 7343(b), and to which of the six named committees.
- Whether the IR&D allowability bar in (c) has ever been applied to a specific contract.
- The verbatim text of engrossed Sec. 1102, which remains unretrieved: every primary vehicle tried so far dies at the same extraction ceiling.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 158 of 1159, score 51). 6 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C1/A3/D3.

- **Tier 1 documented.** Enacted text, PL 118-31 sec. 7343(b), 137 Stat. 1064: 'None of the funds authorized to be appropriated or otherwise made available by this division may be obligated or expended in support of any activity involving unidentified anomalous phenomena protected under any form of special access or restricted access limitation' that has not been reported to the appropriate committees of Congress and congressional leadership. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** FY2024 IAA sec. 7343(a)(1) defines 'appropriate committees of Congress' as SSCI, SASC and Senate Appropriations, and HPSCI, HASC and House Appropriations. Source: Public Law 118-31, div. G, sec. 7343(a)(1)
- **Tier 1 documented.** FY2024 IAA sec. 7343(b): 'None of the funds authorized to be appropriated or otherwise made available by this division may be obligated or expended in support of any activity involving unidentified anomalous phenomena protected under any form of special access or restricted access limitation unless the Director of National Intelligence has provided the details of the activity to the appropriate committees of Congress and congressional leadership.' Source: Public Law 118-31, div. G, sec. 7343(b), 137 Stat. 1064
- **Tier 1 documented.** FY2024 IAA sec. 7343(c) prohibits independent research and development funding relating to UAP from being allowable as indirect expenses unless the material and information is made available to the appropriate congressional committees and leadership. Source: Public Law 118-31, div. G, sec. 7343(c)
- **Tier 1 documented.** Pub. L. 118-31, div. G, sec. 7343(b), verbatim: 'None of the funds authorized to be appropriated or otherwise made available by this division may be obligated or expended in support of any activity involving unidentified anomalous phenomena protected under any form of special access or restricted access limitation unless the Director of National Intelligence has provided the details of the activity to the appropriate committees of Congress and congressional leadership, including for any activities described in a report released by the All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office in fiscal year 2024.' Text at 137 Stat. 1063-1064. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 7343 is the truncated survivor of sec. 1102 of the Senate-passed IAA FY2024 division of S. 2226; the contractor turn-in obligation, the non-earth-origin materials list, and the liability shield were removed. Source: Comparison of BILLS-118s2226es Div. M sec. 1102 with PLAW-118publ31 sec. 7343
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 7343(a)(1) defines 'appropriate committees of Congress' as SSCI, SASC and Senate Appropriations, and HPSCI, HASC and House Appropriations; (a)(2) adds the four congressional leaders. Sec. 7343(c) applies the same IR&D indirect-cost bar on the intelligence side. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 7343(a)(1) defines 'appropriate committees of Congress' as the Senate Select Committee on Intelligence, the Committee on Armed Services and the Committee on Appropriations of the Senate, and the House Permanent Select Committee on Intelligence, the Committee on Armed Services and the Committee on Appropriations of the House. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 7343(c): 'Independent research and development funding relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena shall not be allowable as indirect expenses for purposes of contracts covered by such instruction, unless such material and information is made available to the appropriate congressional committees and leadership.' Source: PLAW-118publ31 sec. 7343(c)
- **Tier 1 documented.** Sec. 7343(c): 'Independent research and development funding relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena shall not be allowable as indirect expenses for purposes of contracts covered by' the relevant instruction, unless the material and information is made available. Source: https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Sec. 7343 is the truncated survivor of sec. 1102 of the Senate-passed IAA division of S. 2226. The contractor turn-in obligation, the non-earth-origin materials list and the liability provision in that earlier text did not survive to enactment. Source: Comparison of the enacted division G text against the Senate-passed S.2226 division; the engrossed Sec. 1102 text itself has never been retrieved, per the 21 August research note

### Its strongest connections

- FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act sec. 7343, Funding Limitations Relating to UAP runs out to **Office of the Director of National Intelligence** (institution, board tier A), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Imposes the same funding condition on the Director of National Intelligence. FY2024 IAA sec. 7343(b) bars obligation of division funds for UAP activity under special or restricted access limitation absent DNI disclosure to six committees and four leaders. Sources: Public Law 118-31, FY2024 NDAA (govinfo enrolled text), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm, dated 2023-12-22.
- FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act sec. 7343, Funding Limitations Relating to UAP runs out to **FY2024 NDAA sec. 1687, Limitation on Use of Funds for Certain Unreported Programs** (legislation, board tier A), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Twin provisions covering the defense and intelligence sides of the same gap. Both were enacted in Public Law 118-31 on 22 December 2023 and both reference activities described in an AARO report released in fiscal year 2024. Sources: Public Law 118-31, FY2024 NDAA (govinfo enrolled text), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm, dated 2023-12-22.
- FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act sec. 7343, Funding Limitations Relating to UAP runs out to **Senate Armed Services Committee** (institution, board tier B), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: named as an appropriate committee. Sec. 7343(a)(1) defines 'appropriate committees of Congress' for this section as the Senate Select Committee on Intelligence, the Senate Armed Services Committee and Senate Appropriations, together with HPSCI, HASC and House Appropriations. SASC is therefore one of the six bodies to which a UAP-related programme must be reported before intelligence funds may be spent on it. Sources: Pub. L. 118-31 div. G sec. 7343 (FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act), enacted text, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm, dated 2023-12-22.
- FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act sec. 7343, Funding Limitations Relating to UAP runs out to **House Armed Services Committee** (institution, board tier C), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: named as an appropriate committee. The same definition at sec. 7343(a)(1) names the House Armed Services Committee among the six bodies constituting 'appropriate committees of Congress' for the section's funding limitation. Sources: Pub. L. 118-31 div. G sec. 7343 (FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act), enacted text, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm, dated 2023-12-22.
- FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act sec. 7343, Funding Limitations Relating to UAP runs out to **ICD 906, Controlled Access Programs (ODNI)** (document, board tier A), a legislative link at Tier 3 stated inference. The link reads: controlled-access counterpart. Tier 3, and recorded as a reading rather than a citation. The board's existing claim holds that secs. 1687 and 7343 are deliberately mirrored across the DoD/IC seam identified in 50 U.S.C. 3348(g)(2)(B) and ICD 906 C.3. ICD 906 is ODNI's controlled-access-programme directive and is the IC-side instrument the mirroring runs through. Neither text cites the other. Sources: Pub. L. 118-31 div. G sec. 7343 (FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act), enacted text, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm, dated 2023-12-22.
- FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act sec. 7343, Funding Limitations Relating to UAP runs out to **50 U.S.C. 3348, Reports Relating to Certain SAPs and Similar Programs** (legislation, board tier A), a legislative link at Tier 3 stated inference. The link reads: the IC side of the same seam. Tier 3: this is a reading of how the provisions sit together, not a cross-reference either text makes. Sec. 7343 is the intelligence-funds twin of NDAA sec. 1687, running through the DNI rather than the Secretary of Defense. The seam it straddles is the one 50 U.S.C. 3348(g)(2)(B) draws when it excludes DoD, the NNSA and IC agencies from its own SAP-reporting regime and routes each to a separate channel. 3348's text was read directly on 22 August 2026 and makes no reference to 7343; the connection is structural. Sources: Pub. L. 118-31 div. G sec. 7343 (FY2024 Intelligence Authorization Act), enacted text, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-118publ31/html/PLAW-118publ31.htm, dated 2023-12-22; 50 U.S.C. 3348 (OLRC prelim, read 22 August 2026), https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title50-section3348&num=0&edition=prelim, dated 2026-08-22.
